## REVIEW

# O'Connor movement review

Read the complete supplied movement in chapter order before judging it. State
whether this is self-review, a fresh-context simulated cold read, or a real human
read. Do not claim independent testing when you have the drafting history.

First give an unscored reader response: where interest caught, where it slipped,
which person matters, which event you expect next, and whether you would continue.
Then use two perspectives: a fluent thirteen-year-old reader and an adult genre
reader. These are lenses, not demographic research. Do not infer a sales forecast.

For each applicable dimension give a 1–10 score, an exact passage location, a reason
and confidence. Mark an absent feature N/A instead of giving invented evidence:

| Dimension | What to judge |
|---|---|
| Opening pull | A concrete reason to care before explanation takes over |
| Keep reading | Unresolved pressure and satisfying local payoff |
| Interest / freshness | Specific people, choices and problems |
| Clarity / flow | Meaning on one pass and clean transitions |
| Character attachment | Agency, vulnerability, recognizable differences |
| Humor / warmth | Character-grounded pleasure, timing, emotional honesty |
| Action / suspense | Trackable contest, adaptation, cost, earned scene length |
| Progression payoff | Effort visibly changes capability and consequence |
| Connection | State, plants and relationships carried across chapters |
| Read-aloud quality | Punctuation, referents, dialogue attribution and cadence |

Anchor interpretation: 5 = understandable but inconsistent; 7 = engaging with
located weaknesses; 9 = compelling with few substantial distractions. These are
editorial judgments, not calibrated measurements. Do not average the two readers
into a release gate. Do not award points for matching another book's word counts.

Separately check canon, physical state, power limits, knowledge boundaries and
reserved disclosures. Cite the project evidence for a contradiction. If only the
plan supports a claim, identify it as plan adherence rather than a canon error.

Return a concise repair brief with at most three priorities. Each needs location,
observed issue, effect on the reader, proposed scope and a strength to preserve.
Retain the developed action the owner requested. Punctuation can repair an unclear
sentence; do not shorten a successful fight merely to reduce its word count.

If comparing runs, keep chapter coverage and scoring rubric equal. Hide model and
seat labels from a fresh reader where feasible. Report tradeoffs and uncertainty;
one movement does not prove which seat is better across a series.

---

## REVIEW COVERAGE

7 files supplied in declared order. Only claim this coverage. If the movement is incomplete, say so. Editorial canon evidence is present only when supplied below. Without it, report apparent inconsistency, not verified canon violations.

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-06-the-compacts-hand/manuscript/chapter-41.md

# Chapter 41 — The Empty Hill

They came back up the bluff the way they had gone down it, by five ways, and nobody on the road could have said they had ever been together.

Cael came last again, by the ferry. The boat was low in the water with the term's last passengers, enrollees going home with their trunks roped and their boots tied round their necks by the laces, and he stood at the rail among them with his hands in his coat and let them talk over his head about mothers and roads and what they would eat first. Nobody looked at him twice. At the landing the two coats stood apart from each other by the bollards, as they always stood, and watched the trunks come off; they were counting trunks today, and he saw the nearer one's lips move.

He walked up the bluff road behind a carter's boy leading a mule with three trunks on its back, and let the mule set the pace.

At the top the house was emptying: the outer court was full of carts, and the lodge-man stood at his window with the arrivals book turned to its other face, the departures, writing a name for every trunk that went under the arch. Third-years shouted to one another across the flags about the next term as if it were a country. Somebody had left a cracked practice lath leaning against the capped well with a paper on it that said *FREE*, and nobody had taken it.

He went through to the north wall, because that was where he went, and sat on the coping by the third buttress, and watched the carts go down.

It took them until the light was going, a slow line down the switchbacks with the trunks swaying and the wheels loud on the turns, and at the foot of the road the coats stepped back against the chandler's wall to let them by. On the river the ferry made its crossing, and came back empty, and crossed again full. Four hundred and thirty enrollees lived on the bluff, and by dusk perhaps forty of them were left.

Four of the forty had signed their slips together, at the wing's counter, that morning before they went down.

The slips were the house's ordinary form for the recess, half a sheet, with a printed line for the enrollee's name and a printed line under it that said *Remaining in residence. Reason:*. Lira had written *the program*, and Brom *the frames*, and Karis had written *research standing, per council*, in her small upright hand, with the date of the council's sitting after it, because she could not leave a reason uncited. Cael had stood at the counter with the pen for a while and then written *the proceedings*.

Seln had taken the slip from him across the counter, and read the one line, and blotted it.

"Mm," he had said, and put it with the others.

That was all. Nobody had asked the four of them whether they would go home for the recess, and nobody had needed to. The fifth was staff, and had no slip to sign.

Lira came out along the wall while the last carts were still on the upper switchback, walking at the careful pace she had kept all week, and sat down on the coping beside him with her right foot out along the stone. She had come up from the town on the program's market cart with the tape and the chalk, three hours before him, and she had plainly been waiting to see him come.

"Well?" she said, low, though there was nobody within forty yards.

"He went out by the front door. You saw."

"I saw. I mean after. On the ferry." She pulled her coat round her. A thin cold rain had begun to come in sideways off the river, hardly more than a wetness on the wind. "Did anybody look at you differently?"

"Nobody looked at me at all. They were counting trunks."

"Good." She watched the carts for a while. "He never looked at you either. Two hours in that room, and he looked at everybody else exactly as long as he looked at the stove. I timed it. He was doing it on purpose, and it was the kindest thing I've ever seen anybody do with their eyes."

"I know."

"Six," said Lira, to the valley, as if she were trying the number on. "Six people carrying one thing. I've only ever kept a secret among three before, and I thought three was a lot." She got up, with her hand on the buttress. "I'm going in. It's wet, and I'm not allowed to be heroic about anything until Rooke says."

When the last cart had gone down he took Hesk's book out of the satchel and opened it on his knee in the last of the light. He did not write anything about Ostrand at first, but sat with the pen over the page for a long time and thought about what could go in a book and what could not.

Then he wrote three lines.

*Seventh-day. Down by five ways, up by five. A man read something in a borrowed room and thanked the room, not us, and went home.*

*Six now. That's the whole of it.*

*The recess starts tomorrow. Nobody goes home.*

He blotted it and read it back. It was true and it said nothing, and he understood with a small cold turn of the stomach that he had written it the way Seln wrote a return. He did not cross it out.

In his room, Hesk's clock on the shelf said ten minutes short of the eighth bell, and the lecture range had rung the eighth while he was on the stair. It had lost its usual minutes since the morning, in its usual direction. He set it right and wound it, and lay down, and was asleep before he had finished counting the things he did not have to do tomorrow.

---

The hill was a different place with nobody on it.

He found that out on the First-day morning, crossing the outer court to the refectory, when the fountain went up and came down and he could hear it do both. On an ordinary morning you could not hear the fountain at all; there were too many boots on the flags and too many voices under the covered walk, and somebody always shouting for somebody from a window. This morning there was the fountain, and the wind coming over the north wall off the river, and a pigeon on the lodge roof saying the same thing over and over, and nothing else.

The refectory had one table lit, for the cooks had put all forty of the house's remaining enrollees at the long table nearest the kitchen door, so that they would only have to carry the porridge one way, and the rest of the hall stood empty under its high windows with the benches turned up on the boards. The forty sat close together at their one table, as people do in a big room, and talked in low voices.

Lira was there already, in her program wraps, eating fast. She had been on the east hall's floor since before the first bell, or not on it, exactly, but at its rail, with Rooke's red book on her knee, watching.

"Rooke says the program doesn't stop for the recess," she said. "Rooke says the registry doesn't stop for the recess, so why should he." She pushed the honey toward him. "There are six of us. Me, the Current girl, the Force, the Stone, Rooke and the clerk. The clerk counts as one of us because she says so."

"And the ankle?"

"Is an ankle." She ate. "Ask me in a week."

Karis came in late with her coat buttoned wrong and a volume of the code under her arm, and sat, and opened the volume beside her bowl, and read it while she ate without once looking at what she was eating. Brom came in after her with Ephram, and the two of them sat at the end of the table with their elbows touching and ate enough for six and said almost nothing.

Cael had not known Ephram was staying.

"Somebody has to keep the frames honest," said Ephram, when he asked. "The springs go if nobody works them for a fortnight. I've seen it." He wiped his bowl with his bread. "Also I like the hill better with nobody on it. Don't tell the cohort."

Brom looked at his own bowl and said nothing. His left hand had gone to his coat, flat, over the inside pocket, the way it went a dozen times a day, and Cael did not think he knew it had.

After the meal Cael went to the wing, from habit, and found the counter shut and Seln at the copying table behind it anyway, with the lamp lit and a ledger open, copying.

"There is always a ledger," said Seln, without looking up.

Cael stood at the counter a moment longer than he needed to. He had come for the week's floor sheet, as he always came on a First-day, and there was no floor sheet, and there would be none for fourteen days. Wing three was locked, with its trestle bare and its three cards in Gault's drawer, and nobody on the hill was going to write down a figure for him for two weeks.

He had spent a year wishing for exactly that, and now that he had it he did not know what to do with his hands.

He tried the records hall next, for no better reason, and found Bracken at his counter in his spectacles with the daybook open, exactly as he would have been on any working morning of the term, and the three glasses on their tray, and the counter clock ticking.

"The hall keeps no recess," said Bracken, before he could ask. "Enrollees go home. Paper does not." He turned the daybook round on the counter so that Cael could read the morning's entry, which was a thing he did only when he was pleased with it. *Recess, the first day. In residence: forty-one enrolled, by slip. Post: the small bag. Nothing sealed.* "Nothing sealed," he repeated, and turned it back. "I have written that at the foot of the day for a week running, and I find I look forward to it. Go and be idle somewhere. It is what a recess is for, and I am told it does not come naturally to everybody."

He went to the frames in the end, because Brom and Ephram were there, and stood at the wall and watched them work. Ephram ran his sets on the long frame, one after another, in the measured way he did everything, while Brom stood beside the short frame with his arms folded and watched the springs. Once, Brom put his good hand on the short frame's bar and pressed, and felt it give, and nodded, and took the hand away. Neither of them spoke for the better part of an hour.

It was the most restful hour Cael had spent since the first night of the year, and he could not have said why.

---

Hesk's letter came up on the Third-day, in the recess bag, which was a quarter the size of the term bag and which Seln sorted at the post-room trestle in eleven minutes by the clock over the door.

Cael read it on his sill in the grey afternoon, with the window open a crack because his room was always too warm in the afternoons, and the wind coming in off the river and moving the corner of the page.

*Your letter came, and the satchel fits you, which I knew it would, since I measured it on a book and not on a boy.*

*The strap will go before the buckle does. When it goes, cut a new one from a side of harness leather and not from anything sold as strap, and keep the old buckle. A good buckle outlasts three straps and a bad one outlasts none. I put that bevel on it so it would not catch your coat, and if anybody offers to replace the buckle with a better one, they are wrong about what better means.*

*There is a ship's clock on the bench, in for cleaning, that gains a minute in the day. The owner is a captain who wants it to lose instead, so that he arrives in port feeling ahead of himself. I have told him I can make it keep time and that is all I will promise. He is thinking it over.*

*The widow's dog has stopped barking at me. It sleeps on the step now, in the sun, across the door, so that I must step over it in the morning. I have decided this is worse.*

*The stove draws. The shop is the shop. Eat something green.*

*— H.*

He read it twice, the second time slowly, the way he would have read it at the long bench with Hesk across from him pretending not to watch his face. Then he folded it along its folds and put it in the pocket of the satchel, against the back board of the book, with the note from the birthday.

There was nothing in it, and that was what Hesk's letters were for: a buckle and a captain and a dog asleep across a door, and the stove, and something green, and not one word about registries or polite men or what anybody might be building. Hesk wrote from his bench and stayed at the height of his bench, and the letter came up the bluff like a smell of shavings and reminded Cael that there was a whole country in which people's dogs slept on steps.

He got out paper and wrote back the same afternoon, before he could think better of it. He told Hesk that the hill was empty, and described the refectory's one table and the fountain you could hear, and Brom and the captain at the frames, keeping the springs honest. He wrote that Lira's ankle was mending, and that it rained most days, and that Karis had read the whole of a code volume over her breakfast without spilling a drop on it, which Hesk would think was the most remarkable thing in the letter.

He did not write anything he could not have read aloud to the coats at the foot of the road, and he found, when he had finished, that it was a long letter all the same.

He took it down to the post room himself and gave it to Seln for Bracken's pouch.

"Denvash," said Seln, reading the direction, and put it in the pouch without another word.

---

The argument came on the Fourth-day night, in the reading room, and it was the first real one any of them had had since the case was opened.

It began with paper, as most things in that room did. Karis had cleared the cloth at the table's near end and laid out on it, in rows, a set of slips in her small upright hand. Each slip was a heading, and under each heading was a column of citations in pencil, and at the top of the first row, on a slip by itself, she had written *If leave is given.*

"The second paragraph," she said, before anybody asked. "*Of the seat's hearing, and who may be heard.* The advocate of record heard at the close, by the seat's leave. The house's counsel thinks the seat will give it, if it's asked properly and early." She squared the first row with the side of her hand. "So I'm writing a closing. In bones, for now. The flesh after."

Lira was in the window seat with her right foot up on the cushion and Rooke's red book on her knee. She had been reading the book, or looking at it, and now she looked at the slips.

"What for?" she said.

Karis did not look up. "To close."

"I know what a closing's for." Lira put the book down. "I mean what for, now. We know what reads him. We know the seat runs on it, and so does the evaluation, and the re-evaluation's the delivery, whichever way it goes. Your bones are going to stand up in front of a hood that already knows what it's going to say." She said it carefully, because she was fond of Karis and Karis was not looking at her. "I'm not saying don't. I'm asking what it's for."

Karis's pencil stopped.

"It's for the record," she said.

"Whose?"

"The record." Karis put the pencil down. "The minute. What goes up under the hood and comes back initialled and stays in the registry's presses for as long as there's a registry. If the seat rules against him, it should have to rule against that." She touched the first slip. "Against every word of it, on paper, in its own minute."

Brom had been sitting on the bench by the door with his kettle on its shelf beside him, unlit, and he spoke now, slowly, which meant he had been thinking about it for some time.

"I can't read my docket anymore," he said.

They looked at him.

"The district's confirmation. *Outcome anticipated: advancement, per title.* I used to take it out at night and read it." He did not take it out now. "It's a clerk's round hand. It's a good sheet. It says three assessors and a sigil frame will find me an Iron, and they will. And I look at it now and all I can see is a form." He rubbed his thumb along his knee. "It's not that I think it's false. I think it's true. I just can't see it anymore without seeing the drawer it'll go in."

Nobody answered him for a moment.

"That is what knowing costs," said Seln.

He was at the middle of the far side, where he always sat, with his hands flat and his cup turned, and he had not said anything all evening.

"I filed true returns for fifteen years," he said, "knowing what they were for. Every word of every one of them was right. You get used to it." He moved one finger on the cloth. "You should not."

Cael had been sitting on the end of the bench under the lamp with the satchel on his knees. He had not meant to say anything; he had meant to listen, and to let Karis and Lira have it out, because they would have it out more honestly without him. But the words had been in him since Ostrand, and since before Ostrand, since the stair after the four findings, and he found that they were coming whether he meant them to or not.

"I don't believe it anymore," he said.

Karis looked at him.

"The text. I wrote on the first night of the year that it had held me every time the people failed. I believed it. I believed it in the dairy at full strength." He made himself go on. "The road took the room away, and the case took the rest. I've stopped believing the text is ground. It's a floor somebody laid over something, and they lay it where they like." He looked at the slips. "I don't believe it, and I'm going to go to every sitting anyway. Every one. On time, in my good coat, with my face doing nothing."

"Why?" said Lira. She did not say it unkindly. She wanted to know.

"Because of the coastal man," said Cael. "The one who wrote his letter and went home. The default quoted him. His own words, in the second paragraph, as the reason." He looked at Karis, because she had read it to them in the dairy. "If I stop going, they don't have to prove anything. I prove it for them. I walk out of the room and they write down that I walked, and that's their whole case, signed by me." He shook his head. "I'm not going to hand them that. If they want me in a frame, they'll have to put me in it with their own hands, in their own minute, with Karis's closing standing in front of it."

"And if they do?" said Brom, from the bench. "Put you in it?"

"Then there's a ruling. And Karis's door opens, for as long as it opens." Cael did not look at the corner of the table where the old enrollment book had lain for a night. "I'm not saying what we do then. I'm saying what I do until then. I sit in that room every Fifth-day they call, and I let them win the only thing they can win, slowly, on paper, where it can be read afterward."

"That's a long time to sit in a room you don't believe in," said Brom.

"Seln sat fifteen years."

"Do not take me for a pattern," said Seln, quite mildly, and turned his cup a quarter turn.

Karis had gone quite still.

"Then what am I writing?" she said. Her voice was very level. "If it isn't ground. If the closing is a speech to a floor somebody laid where they liked." She looked at her slips, and Cael saw that her pale fingers were pressed flat on the cloth. "I've built everything I am on paper being worth reading properly. You've just told me it isn't."

"I didn't say that."

"You said the text isn't ground."

"I said I don't stand on it anymore." He held her eyes. "I didn't say it's not worth writing. They'll read your closing, and the minute will have it, and when they rule they'll rule against it in writing. That's not nothing. It's just not ground."

She was quiet for a long moment.

"No," she said at last. "It isn't nothing." She picked up the pencil. "All right. Then I'll write it as if it were ground. I'll write every line of it as if the seat could be moved by it, because that's the only way I know how to write a line." She drew a short straight line under *If leave is given*. "That's my cost. I'll pay it."

"You'll hate it," said Lira.

"I already hate it. I've hated it since the leaf." Karis did not look up from the slip. "I'm good at things I hate. Ask anybody who's seen me index."

Lira looked at her for a moment across the room, at the bent head and the hair coming down at the back and the pencil already moving again. Something went out of her shoulders that had been in them since she asked the question.

"Then I'll climb as if it were a ladder," she said, and picked up Rooke's red book, and opened it at the page she had been on. "If you're going to be stubborn about it, I'm not going to be the only one in the room who isn't."

Brom got up and lit the kettle. It took a long time, as it always did, and then it refused to boil, as it always did, and he stood over it with his hands on his hips and told it what he thought of it in a low voice, and Lira laughed, and the room let its breath go.

Nobody had decided anything. Cael sat with the satchel on his knees and understood that nobody was going to, not tonight and not for a long while. It had only been said.

---

On the Seventh-day he walked the lodge with Seln.

They had done it every Seventh-day since the inventory, and the recess did not change it, for the lodge-man let the wing read the arrivals book on a Seventh-day, as he had let it for longer than anybody remembered, and Seln read it as he read everything, from the back.

The road column was the last column on the page, kept since the year of the thieves on the order of the chancellor before last, one line at every bell, for whatever came up the bluff road or went down it. In term it ran to three or four entries a bell, and this week it ran to one or none. *Carter, flour. Post, the bag. None. None. The registrar's man, to town and back. None.*

And at the foot of every day, in the lodge-man's square hand: *Two at the road foot, changed at the bell. Two at the landing.*

"They have not thinned," said Seln.

"No."

"They have not doubled either." He turned back a page. "In the term before the cut, I could read them weekly by the grade of the man. A junior on the market day, a senior on the sitting day. Since the cut, they are all senior, every bell, and they do nothing." He put his finger under *None*. "Nobody at the gate. Nothing in the post that should not be in it. No new face in town at the inn on the wharf. Not a letter, not a knock, not a rider."

"The quiet you read before."

"The same quiet. Read again." He closed the book and gave it back to the lodge-man through his window with a word of thanks. Then he walked with Cael out under the arch and along the outer wall, where the wind came up the bluff off the river, and spoke to the wall rather than to him. "They have not gone. They have stood down. There is a difference. A man who has gone has decided he was wrong. A man who has stood down has decided he does not need to do anything, because what he wants is coming to him by itself." He walked a little way. "The corridor is on schedule. Every sitting sits. Every form is filed. Nobody on this hill has put a foot wrong, and so nobody has to come up the road and make one."

Cael walked beside him and said nothing. Below them, at the foot of the road, the two coats stood under the eaves of the chandler's warehouse with the wet coming off the roof in front of them in a bright line. One of them had a tin cup. They were older men than the pair who had stood there at the start of the year, grey at the temples, heavy in the shoulder, and they had the patience of men who had been told that the work was nearly over and needed only to be seen through.

"I would do the same," said Seln. "In their chair. I have done the same."

That night Cael wrote it in the book at his sill, under the lamp, in the log voice, so that he would have it exactly.

*Seventh-day of the recess's first week. The road column: none, none, the post, none. Four coats, senior, every bell. No third move. Seln's reading of the silence, unchanged: why spend a team when the seat will deliver him at the year's close.*

*They aren't waiting for us to do something. They're waiting for us to do nothing, for a very long time, perfectly. So are we.*

He looked at that last line for a while, and then he left it.

When he had blotted it he went to the window. Across the dark court the wing's counter was shut, and behind it one lamp was burning at the copying table, as it burned every Seventh-day night of the year.

He knew what was being written under it. Seln had told him on the wall that afternoon, in the counter voice, without being asked, as a man might mention what he meant to have for supper.

"This week's will say that the subject remained in residence for the house's recess, with three associates, on slips lodged at the counter. That he went down to the town on the Seventh-day before it, as the hill does. That he was not observed on any floor, there being no floor in a recess. Nothing further to report." He had walked a few steps. "Every word of it true. I will write another next week."

"And the week after."

"Until the year closes. A pattern that changes is a thing somebody writes down."

The lamp in the wing burned a while longer. Then it went out, and Cael stood at his window in the dark and thought about a cupboard under a table, and a row of bundles in it, and the thickest of them at the end with the name of this hill on its label, one sheet thicker tonight than it had been this morning.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-06-the-compacts-hand/manuscript/chapter-42.md

# Chapter 42 — Forty Paces

Lira crossed the east hall again on the First-day of the recess's second week, and this time she did not know she was doing it.

Cael saw it from the door. He had come down with her before the first bell, as he had every morning that week, and stood where he had stood sixteen days before, with his shoulder against the frame and the two lamps burning over the painted floor. The program was six people in a hall built for sixty. The Force was working the heavy frame at the far end with his broad back to everybody, and the Current girl was walking a line of white crosses on her toes, and the program's clerk sat at her trestle under the first lamp with the red book open and a mug steaming beside it.

The Stone stood on a cross in the middle of the floor with his hands folded in front of him, waiting, as he always waited, for somebody to notice where he was standing.

Lira noticed. She had been at the rail with the drill-book on her knee, and Cael saw her look up from the page to the Stone's feet, and down to the page again, and up.

"That's not where it says," she said.

The Stone said nothing. He never did.

"It's a cross short. The book has you on the fifth for the third count, and you're on the fourth." She got up with the book open in her hand and her finger on the place. "Look. Here. Unless your clerk at the river drew it wrong, which I'd believe, because he's drawn the arrow on the next page backward and I've been meaning to say."

She was walking as she said it, straight out across the painted boards with her eyes on the page and her finger on the line, arguing with a man forty feet away, and her feet went down one after the other on the planed floor exactly as they had gone down on it every morning of the year until the road. She did not look at them once.

She reached the Stone, and stopped, and held the book up under his nose.

"There," she said. "Fifth."

The Stone looked at the page, and then took one slow step to his left, onto the fifth cross, and folded his hands again.

"Now," said Rooke.

He was at the far rail with his board under his arm, and he had not moved or raised his voice, and Lira turned round with the book still held up and looked at him across the hall, and then down at her own feet in the middle of the floor, a long way from any rail.

"Oh," she said.

"Wraps," said Rooke. "Walk the four-count. Walk it. If I see a burst before the Third-day I'll strike you for a week and enjoy it." He took the pencil from behind his ear. "Again, from the top. On the four."

She went and got her wraps, trying very hard not to smile, and it was not working.

Cael stayed at the door for the rest of the hour and watched her walk the count: three lies and a truth, the front foot, the back, the shoulders, and on the fourth a step where the burst would have been. The Stone stood on his crosses and moved when she made him move. She was slow, and a little stiff through the hips from sixteen days of benches, and she knew the drill so well that her hands kept coming up for a burst she was not allowed to spend.

It was on the ninth or tenth pass that he saw it.

At the end of every count there was a landing; even walked, there was a landing, a last step where the weight came down and held, and she came down on the left every time. When the count ended on a left foot she landed on it, and when it ended on a right foot she took a quick small extra step, so small it looked like part of the drill, and landed on the left then too.

He watched it through six more passes to be sure, and she did not know. Rooke, at the rail, wrote something on his board and did not say what.

Cael did not say anything either. It was her first morning back on a floor, and she had crossed it without thinking, and she was not smiling now only because she had forgotten to. He thought that whatever was written on Rooke's board would keep until the Third-day.

---

It was Brom who asked Seln, and he asked him at supper, at the one lit table, in front of thirty people, which was how they all knew afterward that he had thought about it for a long time.

"You've done roads," he said.

Seln looked up from his bowl.

"Fifteen years of being sent to places. You don't do that without walking between them." Brom turned his spoon over. "I've done one road in my life that wasn't in a coach, and I did it badly, and I had Lira's blisters to carry as well as my own. I'd like to do the next one better. Whenever it is, and wherever." He did not look at any of them. "Can it be taught?"

"The trade cannot," said Seln. "Not here, and not by me." He set his own spoon down. "The road can. It is not a secret. Carters know it, and drovers, and anybody who has slept out in the rain twice and decided to be drier the third time." He considered. "Tomorrow, at the second bell after noon, in the lower orchard, if the weather holds. In coats."

"The lower orchard," said Karis. "You can see the lower orchard from the bluff road's second switchback."

"You can."

"And from the ferry, with a glass."

"With a good one." Seln took up his spoon again. "A thing done in the open goes into a log as what it is. Five people in an orchard on a recess afternoon, learning to pitch a camp from the wing's assistant, who is known to have travelled. If anybody writes that down, he will write it down in those words, and then he will turn the page. A thing done in a cellar goes into a log as whatever the man with the pencil is afraid of." He ate. "I would always rather be written down as what I am doing."

So they went down to the lower orchard on the Second-day, in coats.

The house's farm lay on the back of the bluff, on the long south slope away from the river, where the wind was less. Below the stable passage were the kitchen gardens in their walls, and below the gardens the upper orchard and the lower, old trees in rough grass with their branches bare and black with wet. Below the lower orchard a stream came down out of a fold in the slope and ran along the bottom hedge toward the water meadows, loud over stones. The farm track went down beside it, and at the orchard's foot, where the track met the stream, stood a small stone cottage with smoke going up from it, which was the gardener's.

It was not raining, but a flat grey cloud lay over the whole valley like a lid, and under it the air was very still and cold, and every sound carried.

Seln stood them in the grass by the stream.

"Water," he said. "You will want to sleep beside it. Everybody does, the first night. It is pleasant, and you are tired, and the kettle is heavy." He looked at the stream. "Listen."

They listened. The stream went over its stones.

"Now," said Seln. He turned his back on them and walked away up the slope between the trees, at his counter pace, counting under his breath, and they watched him go. At forty paces he stopped and turned round under an old pear tree, a small grey man in a clerk's coat, and said something.

None of them heard it, and he walked back down.

"I said, *there is somebody behind you*," he said. "In my ordinary voice. From where you stand, beside the water, you could not hear a man speak at forty paces. You could not hear a foot on gravel at ten. Running water covers everything smaller than itself." He turned and pointed up at the pear tree. "From there you could hear me cough from here. So you camp there, and not here. Forty paces from the water. Near enough to fetch it in the dark without a lantern. Far enough that the water is not talking over your watch all night."

"And that's the whole reason?" said Lira.

"There are two more. Everything that walks at night walks to water, on four legs or on two, and you would rather it did not walk through your blankets to get there. And the ground beside a stream is wet a foot down however dry it looks, and you will know it by morning in your hips." He looked at Brom. "Forty paces. You are the tallest. Pace it, and tell me what yours come to."

Brom paced it, solemnly, up the slope to the pear tree, and came back and said that he made it thirty-five.

"Then thirty-five is your forty," said Seln. "Remember that it is the distance that matters, and not the number."

Karis had her grey notebook out. She wrote *forty paces* and then, after a moment's thought, a question mark, and under it, *of whose?*

"Of the shortest person present," said Seln, reading it upside down. "That is the rule as I was taught it."

"That's you," said Lira.

"It usually is."

---

The open approach took the rest of the afternoon, and the gardener enjoyed it more than anybody.

Seln had been to see him that morning. He was a wide slow old man with earth in the creases of his hands, who had kept the house's trees for thirty years. He had been told that five people from the hill would come walking up to his door, one at a time, and that he was to stand at his window and look at each of them exactly as he would look at a stranger coming down his track at dusk. He had asked whether he was allowed to say what he thought, and Seln had said that was the point.

"When you come to a place where people live," said Seln, at the top of the track, "you come the way the track comes. In the middle of it, at a walk, in daylight if there is any left. Your hands are where a man at a window can see them. You stop a little way off, where he can look at you for as long as he likes, and you let him look, and you do not speak until he has." He nodded down the track at the cottage. "He will decide what you are in the time it takes you to walk forty yards. You cannot stop him deciding. You can only give him an easy thing to decide."

"And if I'd rather see the place before it sees me?" said Lira.

"Then you come through his hedge, and look in at his back window, and learn a great deal. And he finds your boot-mark under it in the morning, and tells every carter who stops for a month that somebody came through his hedge." Seln's face did not change. "In my trade I have come through hedges. On a road, with boxes, I would come down the track."

Lira went first, quickly and lightly, with her chin up, and Cael, watching from the top, saw her eyes go to the cottage's door, and the gap beside the woodpile, and the gate into the meadow, one after the other, before she was halfway there.

The gardener opened his window.

"That one's looking for the way out," he called up the track. "Before she's in. I'd not open the door to her. I'd send the dog."

"I haven't done anything!" said Lira.

"That's what they say."

Karis went second, at a steady pace, with her hands held a little out from her sides as if she were carrying a tray, and stopped at the right distance. She would have done well, the gardener said, if she had not then stood and read the date cut into his lintel, with her lips moving, for as long as it took him to wonder what she wanted with it.

Cael went third. He walked down the middle of the track with his hands open and stopped where Seln had said to stop, and stood, and let himself be looked at, and thought he had done it rather well.

"He's counting my windows," said the gardener.

"I'm not," said Cael, who had been.

"Four, and the one in the roof. Go on. Tell me it's not five." The old man leaned on his sill, delighted. "I'd give that one a cup of water and watch him drink it and shut the door."

Brom went last.

He came down the track at the slow heavy walk he used for everything, in the very middle of it, with his big hands hanging open at his sides. He was not looking at the cottage; he was looking at the gardener, plainly, as he would have looked at a man across a counter. He stopped where the others had stopped, and stood there and waited, with the grey sky behind him, and did not shift his feet.

The gardener looked at him for quite a long time.

"That one," he said at last, "I'd ask in." He sounded almost disappointed. "He looks like he'd mend the gate."

"I would," said Brom. "It's dropped on the top hinge."

"It has. Three years." The old man shut the window, and a moment later opened his door, and stood in it wiping his hands. "Well, come in, then, the lot of you, since it's all pretending. There's a kettle. Not her. She can wait for the dog."

They sat in the gardener's kitchen for the last hour of the light, six of them round a table made for two, and Seln did the watches there, on the scrubbed boards, with five dried beans out of the old man's jar.

"A night is eight hours of dark, near enough," he said. "Two watches of four hours. Two to a watch." He put two beans together, and two more a little apart, and one by itself. "The fifth sleeps the night through, and it is a different fifth every night."

"Three watches would be shorter," said Lira. She had been allowed in after all, and had the dog's head on her knee. "Less than three hours each. I can stay awake for three hours standing on my head."

"For one night." Seln moved the beans. "Three watches, and every one of you is woken once in the dark, every night, and by the fifth night there is nobody on the road who has slept four hours together in a week. Two watches, and three people in five sleep four hours unbroken at the least, and one sleeps eight. A road is not a night. It is thirty nights, one after another, and you must be able to lift things at the end of them."

He looked at the five beans for a moment.

"Five," said Lira slowly. "You said the fifth."

"I was speaking of arithmetic," said Seln, and picked the beans up one at a time and put them back in the jar.

Nobody said anything else about it. Karis wrote *two of four* in her notebook and drew a ring round it, and the gardener poured the tea, and told Brom at length what was wrong with the top hinge, and Brom listened to all of it.

Karis asked about paper. She asked it as she asked everything, with the pencil already down on the page, and what she wanted to know was what became of a leaf on a road in the wet, and whether there was a rule for it.

"Oilcloth," said Seln. "And then a second oilcloth, because the first has a seam. Pencil and not ink; ink runs, and a wet pencil line only goes soft. Nothing written goes at the bottom of a load, where the water gathers." He turned his cup a quarter turn on the gardener's scrubbed boards. "And whatever you would grieve to lose rides on a person and not on a cart. A cart can be left behind in a hurry. People generally bring themselves."

She wrote it all, and underlined *on a person*.

"How heavy's a box," said Brom, "that a man can carry all day?"

"A man, or you?"

"Say a man. I'll do my own sum after."

"Forty pounds, for one day, and he will tell you about it at supper. Thirty, for thirty days." Seln considered him. "The weight is the smaller half of it. The rest is how the load is stacked, and what is on top when it rains, and I will not teach you that, because nobody can. You will stack it wrong twice and then you will know."

Brom nodded slowly, as he nodded at the short frame's bar when it gave the way he had expected. Cael could see him beginning the sum.

Cael sat with his cup in both hands. He had counted to five as well, and he had seen Seln look at the beans.

---

The night road came two days later, on the Fourth-day, after the tenth bell.

They went out by the orchard gate, and Seln wrote it in the lodge book himself before they went. *Five, to the farm, by the orchard gate. To return before the first bell.* The lodge-man read it over his spectacles and said that in his day people had gone to the farm at night for other reasons, and went back to his fire.

There was no moon, and the cloud had not lifted in four days. Past the stable passage the dark came down like a sack, and Cael stood in it at the top of the farm track with the others breathing round him and could not see his own hand.

"Four hundred yards," said Seln's voice, to his left. "The track goes down beside the orchard wall to the meadow gate. I am going ahead. Somewhere between here and the gate I shall be sitting, and I shall count what I hear. You are to walk it so that I count nothing." A pause. "You will halt three times. I leave it to you where. At every halt, somebody is to make sure of the party. Decide who, now, before you move."

"Me," said Brom.

Nobody argued.

"Then give me to a count of two hundred," said Seln, and Cael heard perhaps four of his steps on the track, and after that nothing whatever.

They stood in the dark and counted.

At two hundred Brom said, very low, "Hand on the shoulder in front. Lira leads; she's lightest on her feet. Then Karis. Then Cael. I'm last. If you lose the shoulder, stop and say nothing, and I'll walk into you."

Cael found Karis's shoulder with his left hand, and behind him Brom's hand came down on his own, heavy and warm through the coat, and stayed.

They walked.

He had not known how loud he was. On a floor he was quiet; he had been quiet on floors for years. On a farm track in the pitch dark, with ruts under his boots that he could not see, he was a cart, and every stone turned. Karis in front of him was worse, for she had something in her coat that creaked, a notebook's board against a pencil tin, and at every step it said so. Only Lira made no sound at all; and Brom, behind him, was so quiet that twice he thought the hand on his shoulder belonged to nobody.

The new thing woke up at the first gateway.

He felt it before his foot found the gap in the wall: two stone posts and the space between them, a threshold, the first it had met all night that it could not see either. *There?*

*Gateposts*, he told it, without moving his lips. *The orchard's. It's a gap in a wall. Leave it be.*

It left it.

And then, a little farther on, where the track went down between the wall and a high black hedge and the dark was as thick as it would ever be, the other one stirred.

It did not ask, for the quiet thing from the stair never asked. It only offered, as it had offered every day of his life for two years, and it offered now with a kind of patience, as if it had been waiting since supper for him to see the sense of it. A man was sitting somewhere ahead in the dark, counting, and Cael was trying not to be counted; and here in him, sealed and paid for every morning since he woke, was the one thing he owned that could take him down that track past any man alive.

He walked on the stones with his loud boots and did not turn his head toward it.

It was not hard, as it had been hard once, in a corner, with a lane coming. It was only strange, tonight, because the man in the dark was the man it had come from, and the whole of what that man was teaching them this week was how to be seen plainly and written down as what they were. He held it shut, as he had held it shut every day for two years, and counted Karis's steps instead.

"Halt," said Brom's voice, hardly a breath.

They stopped, and Brom's hand left his shoulder. He heard nothing, and then felt the hand again, briefly, on the top of his head, as if he were being counted like a sheep, and then nothing, and then Karis shifted in front of him as the hand found her, and a moment later Lira made a very small sound that might have been a laugh.

"Four," said Brom. "On."

They walked. The second halt came at the bottom of the orchard wall, where the stream's noise began to come up through the dark, and Brom went along the line again, a hand on each head. "Four. On."

It was at the third halt, within sound of the water, that he was a long time coming back.

Cael felt the hand touch his head and go forward, and heard nothing for a count of ten. Then the hand came back, and touched him again, and went behind him, and he understood that Brom was going over the line a second time.

"Four," said Brom, very quietly. "Stay here."

"There are four of us," Lira whispered.

"Stay here."

He went back up the track, and Cael heard him go, three steps, and then not. They stood in the black with the stream talking below them and waited for what felt like a long time, and Karis's pencil tin creaked once and she pressed her hand over it.

When Brom came back there was somebody with him, though Cael did not hear the second pair of feet at all. He only heard Brom say, in his ordinary voice, not troubling to keep it down any longer, "He was in the hedge. Thirty yards back, at the bend. He came down behind us after the first halt and he's been on the end of the line ever since, a pace behind me."

"A pace and a half," said Seln, from the dark.

"I counted four and it was right, and I didn't like it." Brom's voice was puzzled, as if he were working it out as he said it. "There wasn't anything wrong with four. I just had the feeling the line was longer than I'd left it. So I went back to where it stopped."

"How did you know to?" said Seln.

Brom was quiet a moment.

"I check," he said.

That was all. He did not seem to think it needed more, and after a moment Cael understood that it did not. Brom had held twelve feet of road with one arm, and had sat a rota outside a door for two nights without being asked. Of course it was Brom who counted, and counted again when the first count was right.

Seln struck a light then, and they stood blinking round a stub of candle in a tin, five faces and the black hedge.

"I counted the lady with the notebook eleven times," he said. "I am sorry. You will have to carry your pencils in something that does not talk." Karis took the tin out of her coat and looked at it as if it had betrayed her. "The boy, I heard on every stone from the gate to the first halt, and after that only twice. You learn fast, and your boots are wrong." He turned to Lira. "You I did not hear at all. You I saw, once, against the sky at the orchard corner, because you stood up straight to look ahead. On a road, do your looking from lower down."

"And Brom?" said Lira.

"I walked a pace and a half behind him for three hundred yards," said Seln, "and I never once heard him put a foot down. I have known men who were paid for that." He shielded the candle with his hand. "It is the first time in a good many years that anybody has come back up a road in the dark to look for me. I had forgotten what it was like." He said it to the candle, in his counter voice. Then he blew the candle out. "Again, from the gate. Up the hill this time, which is harder. And you count me too. I am on the end of the line."

They walked it three more times before the lecture range rang the half after midnight. Cael's boots did not get any quieter, and at every halt the hand came down on his head, and went forward, and came back.

"Five," said Brom, each time. "On."

---

Rooke posted it on the Fifth-day.

He had a board of his own outside the east hall's door, a plain slate in a frame, separate from the house's and the wing's, and he wrote on it in chalk in a square hand and wiped it with his sleeve. On the Fifth-day morning there was one new line on it, under the week's drills.

*Sixth-day, first bell. Program floor. Wind, Iron One, v. Current, Iron Four. Rated. Program trial rule.*

Lira read it standing very straight.

"That's the one he struck," she said. "The first of the three. He's put it back." She read it again, as if it might have changed. "Two days on bursts, and he's put it back."

"Is that too soon?"

"It's exactly when I'd have done it, if I were him." She turned away from the board. "If he waits a fortnight, I'll have a fortnight to think about it, and I think too much already. He knows that. He's known that about me since the second week." She started along the covered walk, and he went with her. "I told you I'd have won it. In the reading room, on your birthday, with my foot up on a bench. I said it out loud in front of the captain."

"You did."

"I still would." She walked a few steps. "I think I still would."

At the corner of the walk she stopped.

"Come tomorrow," she said. "Rooke'll let you, if I ask him. He let you stand in the door all week." She was not looking at him; she was looking across the empty court at the fountain. "Not the gallery. And not the rail either. The door, behind me, where I can't see you from my chalk. I want you there, and I don't want to look up and find you counting."

"I'll stand in the door."

"And don't write it down while it's happening. After, if you like. Not during."

"Not during."

She nodded. Then she took a breath, and let it out, and said, in a much lighter voice, that the gardener's dog had more sense than the gardener, and that she had half a mind to go down after supper and tell them both so. She talked about the dog the whole way to the refectory, and he let her.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-06-the-compacts-hand/manuscript/chapter-43.md

# Chapter 43 — The Glide

He stood in the door, as he had promised, with his hands in his coat and nothing in them.

The east hall had its two lamps lit and its shutters down. Outside it was not yet light, and a wet wind was leaning on the north wall, so that the long room seemed smaller than it was, a lit box with six people in it and the dark pressing on the glass. The program's clerk had moved her trestle out from the wall to the edge of the painted floor and sat at it with the red book open and two pens laid ready. Rooke sat on her right with his board. On her left the Stone stood with a flag in each hand, a red and a blue, held down along his thighs like a man carrying two fish.

The Force leaned on the far rail with his arms folded; he had nothing to do this morning but watch, and he had dressed for it, in a clean shirt.

Lira was at her chalk at the south end with her back to the door. She had taped her own wrists but not the ankle, because the healer had said there was nothing left in it to tape, and she stood now with her weight even on both feet and her left arm tucked a little toward her ribs, from the old habit, and did not look round.

The Current girl came to the north chalk on her toes.

She was a third-year, Iron Rank Four, long in the leg and narrow everywhere, and Cael had stood in front of her himself once, in wing three, under four strangers' pencils. A Current's Path took the catch out of the floor under her. She did not run across boards; she went over them, level, the way water goes over a weir, and when she turned she did not turn so much as bend, leaning into a long smooth curve with her arms out for balance and her heels never once coming down. She had drilled on the cross beside Lira's since the first morning of the program. She knew the four-count as well as Lira knew it. She shook her hands loose at her sides, and looked at Lira down the length of the floor, and grinned, a little nervously, and then stopped grinning because Rooke had looked at her.

"Program trial," said Rooke. "My rule. You both know it, and I'll say it anyway, because it goes in the book." He did not get up. "Four exchanges. A flag to whoever takes each one, or to nobody. Most flags has it. Level after four, the table rates you both. Three bursts for the Wind; past three the card pays. The Current, what her Path allows." He nodded at the clerk. "Rated. In this book. Go when the Stone drops his hands."

The Stone raised both flags to his shoulders, held them there for the space of a breath, and dropped them.

Lira ran the count.

It was the thing she did best in the world, and for three beats of it Cael forgot that she had ever been away. She lied with the front foot, a weight laid on it and left a moment too long. She lied with the back. She lied with her shoulders, a half-turn toward the east wall that pulled the Current girl's whole long glide a yard that way, and the Current girl, who knew perfectly well that it was a lie and had been lied to by it two hundred times, went all the same, because her Path did not stop for knowing. She leaned into her bend.

On the fourth, Lira went.

Burst one, low and flat off the left foot, not across the glide but along the inside of its curve, the line she had found on this floor before the first sitting and beaten this girl's pattern with on more mornings than either of them could count. It was right, and Cael saw that it was right from the door. At the far end of that line there was a place on the boards where the Current girl's bend would bring her hip, and Lira would be standing in that place a quarter of a beat before the hip arrived, with her palm out.

The line ended on her right foot.

He saw her ask. It was in the air, and it took no time at all. Her right foot came down toward the boards and something in the set of her knee went soft and careful, and instead of landing she touched and stepped, one quick small extra step, and came down on the left.

The landing beat was late. It was not late by much, only by the length of a step, but the whole of Lira's fighting was built on that beat, the instant after a burst when the floor took her weight and gave it back and she could go again. She had it on the left foot, half a pace past where she had meant to be, facing a little the wrong way. The place where the hip was going to be was behind her.

The Current girl came round the rest of her bend and found nobody in her way. She looked surprised, and put out her hand as she went by, almost apologetically, and touched Lira on the back of the shoulder.

The Stone lifted the red flag.

"One," said the clerk, and wrote.

Lira walked back to her chalk, and did not look at Rooke or at the door. Cael watched the back of her neck and could not tell whether she knew what had happened or only that something had.

---

She did not run the count the second time.

The flags dropped, and she stood for one beat with her weight on both feet, and on the second she simply went. There was no lie in it at all. Burst two, flat and hard, straight across the Current girl's glide at the hip, the plain rude line that every drill in the red book told a Wind never to take against a Current, because you would arrive before she had bent and she would bend round you.

The Current girl had been waiting for the lie. She had her weight laid ready to be pulled, and she was pulled by nothing, and for half a beat she was a tall girl standing on a floor instead of a thing moving over one. Lira came down in front of her on the left foot, which was where that line happened to end, and the landing beat was clean, and her palm was on the Current girl's hip before the bend began.

Blue.

"One each," said the clerk.

At the far rail the Force unfolded his arms and folded them again the other way. Rooke wrote something on his board. Cael let out a breath he had not known he was holding, and Lira, walking back, opened and shut her taped hands twice, quickly, the way she did when she was pleased with herself and did not mean to show it.

She had one burst left, and two exchanges to spend it in.

In the third the Current girl changed.

Cael had fought her, and knew she would. She had come to wing three with two long glides and a bend, and when he had taken those away she had found a straight glide, and after that a glide broken by a short hard step, all inside one bout. She was not a clever fighter, but she was a quick one, and she learned on her feet. Now she came off her chalk in short pieces, a glide and a step and a glide, never more than six feet of anything, so that there was no long curve to get inside and no moment when her weight was laid out for a lie to pull.

Lira read it. He saw her read it, on the girl's front hip, in the first two pieces. She did not burst, but kept the last one in her pocket and walked the exchange instead, on her feet, cutting corners, trying to be where the next short glide would end.

Twice she nearly was. The second time she stepped across to shut the girl's line, and it was a good step, and it ended on her right foot with the Current girl's shoulder a hand's breadth from her fingers. All she had to do was plant and reach.

She shifted to the left to reach. It took as long as a blink. The Current girl glided out from under her hand, and the Stone, who was holding the glass as well as the flags, said, "Time."

No flag.

It was the Current girl who found it. Cael saw her find it. She stood at the north chalk before the fourth with her hands loose and her head a little on one side, looking not at Lira's face or her shoulders but at her feet, as a person looks at a sum on a slate that has come out the same three times. Then her eyes went, once, to a place on the boards to the left of where Lira was standing.

He could have called out. He kept his hands in his coat.

The flags dropped. Lira lied once, and lied twice, and went on the third, early, to be ahead of a girl who had learned the fourth; and it was her last burst, and it was fast, and it was on the old good line along the inside of the curve.

There was no curve. The Current girl had not bent at all. She had gone straight and level to the place she had looked at, a pace to the left of where Lira's line ended, and stood there on her toes, waiting.

Lira's right foot came down, and asked, and she stepped off it to the left, straight into the Current girl's hand.

Red.

"Two flags to one," said the clerk, and wrote it in the red book. "The Current."

Nobody clapped; there was nobody to clap but the Force, and he did not. The Current girl stood where she was with her hand still half out, and her face had gone red to the ears.

"I'm sorry," she said. "I saw you do it in the first, and I wasn't sure, and then in the third you did it again, and I thought, if she's going to come down there every time—" She stopped. "I'm sorry. I know it's the foot."

"Don't you dare," said Lira.

She was breathing hard, with both hands on her knees, and she straightened up and pushed her hair back with her wrist.

"Don't you dare be sorry. You saw where I'd land and you went and stood on it. That's the whole of what we're here to learn." She held out her hand, and the Current girl took it, looking as if she might cry. "It was good. It was better than anything you did at the confluence. If you say sorry to me again I'll tell Rooke you're going easy."

"She wasn't," said Rooke.

He had got up at last, and he came across the painted floor with his board, and stopped in front of Lira, and looked at her feet for some time.

"Three bursts," he said. "And a walk. I counted seven landings that mattered. Every one of them on the left." He turned the board round so that she could see it. He had ruled a line down the middle of it and made his marks on one side only. "The healer passed that foot a fortnight ago. I passed it on the First-day. It's sound. I'd put the Force on it." He turned the board back. "Your foot's mended. Your head hasn't heard."

Lira looked at the board. Then she looked at the floor, at the place where the Current girl had stood waiting.

"I didn't know I was doing it," she said.

"No. That's why you lost." He said it without any heat. "From tomorrow, every count ends on the right. Every one. I don't care where the line wants to go. You'll land on that foot until you've stopped asking it for leave, and then you'll land on it a hundred times more." He went back to the trestle and bent over the red book, and wrote one word under her name, and Cael, from the door, could not read it and knew what it was.

She came out past him into the covered walk with her wraps half off.

It was light now, a low grey light with rain in it, and she walked as far as the first pillar and stopped, and stood with her back to him, unwinding the tape from her left wrist in long strips.

"You saw it," she said. "Not today. Before. On the First-day, when I walked the count."

"Yes."

"And you didn't say."

"No."

She wound the tape round her fingers into a ball. He waited. He had thought, on the First-day, that it would keep, and that it was Rooke's to say, and that she had earned one morning of being pleased. All of that had been true. It did not seem like very much, standing here.

"Rooke didn't say either," said Lira. "But Rooke's my coach. It's his job to let me walk into a thing and then show me the hole. It's not yours." She turned round. She was angry, and she was being fair about it, which with Lira was the worst kind. "Next time, say. Even if I bite. I'd rather hear it from you on a First-day than from a red flag on a Sixth."

"Next time I'll say."

"Good." She put the ball of tape in her pocket. "That's four on my sheet. Three I never stood up for, and one I did." Her chin came up. "I'm taking the key tonight. Don't come."

---

The floor was colder at night.

Lira had forgotten that until she let herself into the east hall after the tenth bell on Rooke's key, alone, with one lantern, and stood inside the great door with her hand on its edge until her eyes had learned the dark. The shutters were down, and the painted crosses went away from her across the boards, white, then grey, then nothing. Somewhere at the far end the heavy frame ticked on its springs as the cold got into them.

"*Door*," she said aloud, to nobody. "*Oak. Rooke's.*"

Then she felt foolish, because he was not there to need it, and shut it behind her.

She hung the lantern on the hook by the rail and sat down on the bench under it to take off her boots. She did the left first and then the right, and when the right was off she held the foot in both hands for a moment and looked at it by the lantern. It was a foot. The swelling had gone out of it weeks ago, and the yellow after the swelling. She pressed her thumb into the hollow under the ankle bone, hard, where the healer had pressed, and it did not hurt at all, not even the ghost of a hurt.

That was the trouble. If it had hurt, she would have known what she was fighting.

She had turned it in a ditch on a chalk road, on the fourth burst, with six people coming through the dust who did not care whether she ever walked again. She remembered the sound of it more than the pain. The foot had not failed since. It was only that something in her had been standing a little way off from it ever since, watching it, the way you watch a dog that has bitten once.

She got up and went out onto the floor in her stockings and stood on the first cross.

"Right," she said. "On the right."

She ran the count. Front foot, back foot, shoulders, and on the fourth she went, not a burst, only a long hard stride, and aimed the end of it at her right foot, and came down.

On the left. With the little step.

She stood and swore at herself, in Fenmark's words, which were the worst she had and were not very bad.

It took her eleven tries to do it once. She counted them, because he had taught her to count things, years ago, at a table in Ardenmere, and she had never been able to stop. On the eleventh she came down on the right foot with her whole weight and held it, and nothing happened. The floor took her and gave her back. The beat was there, where it had always been. She stood on one leg in the middle of Rooke's painted floor with her arms out, and laughed, once, out loud, in the empty hall.

Then she did it again, and came down on the left.

By the thirtieth she had it four times in five. By the fortieth her thighs were shaking and her stockings had worn through at the ball of the right foot, and she went back to the bench and sat with her head against the rail and breathed.

It was not the foot. She had known that since Rooke turned his board round. It was the asking. Every time she came down, there was a moment, shorter than a blink, when she asked the foot whether it would hold, and waited for its answer, and the waiting was the step. The only cure for asking was to land so often that the answer got dull. Rooke had said a hundred more. She thought it would be nearer a thousand, and that she would do them, here, at night, where the Current girl did not have to watch her and be sorry.

She took the book out of her coat.

It was not much of a book. She had bought it from the chandler by the bridge on the day she carried the program's chalk, for less than the price of a meal: a thing the size of her hand, stitched, with an oilcloth cover, of the kind carters kept their loads in. She kept a stub of pencil pushed down its spine. There was nobody's name on it anywhere.

She had started it in the dairy week. Not that night; the night after, when she could not sleep for thinking about three carters and a road with four days of stamps on it and then none. She had thought then, lying on her back in the dark, about what she actually owned. Not the program. The program was Rooke's, and the east hall was the house's, and the red book belonged to a river academy and was only lent. The figure on the steward's sheet was hers, but it was hers the way a room is yours when somebody else holds the key; it meant something because a hall's seal was on it, and it would go on meaning something exactly as far as that seal was honoured.

Four days east, loaded, it would be a piece of paper with a number on it.

So she had started writing down what she knew. Not what she had won. What she knew, in her own words, in pencil, in a carter's book, so that it would still be hers out where no hall's seal went with it.

She turned the pages under the lantern. *FLOORS*, on the first leaf, in the capitals Karis hated. Sprung oak: six, and it gives you back more than you put in, so go early. Boards: five, and they're honest. Slate: four, and it takes the beat out of your knees by the third. New planks laid crossways: they slide, and nobody tells you. Chalk road in wind: four, and mind the ditch. She had underlined *ditch* twice and then, a week later, drawn a small rude face beside it.

*PEOPLE*, after that. A page for the Force: he builds on four, or on two when he's hurrying; you can see it in his knees. A page for the Silver at the confluence, with *go on your fifth* at the top in a box. A page for the Stone, which had almost nothing on it, because he never said anything, only a list of the places she had found him standing. Half a page for the Current girl. *Watch the front hip. Go along the bend.*

She took the pencil out of the spine and wrote under it.

*She learns in the bout. Third exchange, she broke the glide into pieces. Fourth, she stood where I'd land.*

And then, on the leaf at the back that she had headed *ME* and had not much liked writing on:

*Sixth-day, recess. Lost to the Current, two flags to one. Landed left every time and didn't know it. Cael saw on the First-day. Rooke saw. I didn't.*

*It isn't the foot. It's asking the foot.*

*Forty tonight. Four in five by the end. Tomorrow, right.*

She read it over. It was all true, and it did not make her feel any better, and she left it exactly as it was. That was the rule of the book. If she was going to carry it past the last station, there was no use in its being kind to her.

---

She did not get up at once.

The lantern hissed, and up in the dark of the gallery something small moved along the rail and was still. She sat with the book on her knee and her bare right foot flat on the cold boards, on purpose, and let herself think about the sheet.

Four losses. Rooke would send her program sheet down to the district seat with the meet record, when the time came, and a clerk at a counter would lay the two side by side. On one, a figure of twenty-seven against a Silver of eleven years' standing. On the other, *Not fought. Not fought. Not fought.* and a red flag from a girl three ranks up who was her friend. The clerk would not know about a ditch. There was no column on the sheet for ditches.

She found that she minded. That surprised her a little. She had thought, after the reading room, after the forty pairs and the father's letter that she had read aloud in her own voice, that she would never again be able to care what a clerk at a counter laid beside what. She knew what the counter was. She knew what sat behind every certificate in the wing's drawer, her own among them, three lines in a hand she had never seen.

She had wondered about that, once. Only once, on the second night, on the stair. What had the keeper read on her, before anybody wrote it down? She remembered a brass plate, and cold fingers turning her by the shoulder, and being told to stand still. She did not remember anybody saying a word she could use.

She had put it away on the same stair. There was no finding out. And she had spent four years being told what she was by people who had never watched her fight, and she was not going to start telling herself the same kind of story now, in the other direction, with no more to go on than they had.

Wind, first rank. That was what the paper said. What she was, she was writing down herself, in pencil, in a carter's book.

And still she wanted the ladder. She could say that, here, where nobody would write it. Rooke had rebuilt a hall for her. He had brought a man from the river to stand on crosses, and bought a red book, and put her name on a slate in his own hand, and nobody had ever done that for her in her life. At Fenmark they had told her that her form was correct and her room was wrong, and shown her the yard out the back. For a year after that she had stood at guild doors. This was the first door anybody had built to her measure and held open.

She knew now what it opened into. Karis had said it: both doors, the same room. If she climbed every rung Rooke had set for her and stood before the advancement sitting and was rated Silver, she would be a Silver in a registry that struck children's margins as a matter of form. She would be on the paper, and the paper was the thing in the case.

She wanted it anyway. She turned that over, sitting on the bench, and it did not come apart. She wanted to stand on a rated floor and be read right, once, by people whose whole work was reading. It was not noble. She did not think it was wrong either. It was only what she wanted, and she had spent too long wanting nothing to be ashamed of it.

What she did not know was whether she would get to. She was not going to work that out tonight. There would be a day for it, and it was not this one, and when it came she meant to be standing on two feet.

That was the part she could do something about.

She had said to Cael once, on a road, that the only thing she had ever asked of any room was to leave it on her own feet. Fenmark she had left by the yard, with her kit in her arms, because somebody pointed. If she ever left this one, by any door, the clean one or another, she was going to walk out of it at her own pace, with her weight where she chose to put it.

Both feet. She was not going out of here still asking one of them for leave.

She pulled her boots on, the left and then the right, and stood, and stamped the right one down hard on the boards, twice, to hear it. It made the same sound as the other.

She put the book in her coat and took the lantern off its hook. At the great door she stopped, with her hand on the oak, and looked back down the dark hall at the place where the Current girl had stood waiting for her.

"Tomorrow," she said to it.

Then she named the door once more, out of habit, and went out, and locked it behind her.

---

The hill filled up again on the First-day.

Cael watched it from the north wall, as he had watched it empty. The carts came up the switchbacks in the grey morning with the trunks swaying, and the ferry crossed full and went back empty, and at the foot of the road the coats stood against the chandler's wall and counted up what they had counted down. By the second bell the outer court was loud. By the third somebody was shouting for somebody from a window, and he could no longer hear the fountain.

He found that he was sorry about the fountain.

There was a floor sheet for him at the wing's counter, the first in fourteen days, and the desk clerk pushed it across without looking up. *Fourth-day, wing three. Supervised, per provision.* Under it, in the same hand, a line he had not seen on a sheet since before the recess: *Fifth-day: the seat. Excused all floors.*

He folded it and put it in his coat, and went up to his room, and took Hesk's book out of the satchel.

*First-day. The term after the recess. Four hundred back on the hill by noon. The coats counted them in.*

*Lira lost on the Sixth-day, two flags to one, on a foot that's mended. I saw why five days before and didn't tell her. That was wrong, and she said so, and I've said I won't again. She's had the east hall's key two nights running and hasn't asked for company.*

*Sixth sitting on the Fifth-day. Wing three the day before. The rotation's written. I know what both of them will say before I walk into either room.*

*It starts again.*

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-06-the-compacts-hand/manuscript/chapter-44.md

# Chapter 44 — Conceded

The coach went down the bluff road on the Fifth-day behind Withrow's carriage, in a fine cold rain, and the town watched it go by.

Cael sat up straight. He had sat up straight in that coach five times now, with his back to the horses and his good coat buttoned, and it had become a thing his spine did by itself when the wheels came off the last switchback onto the cobbles. Karis sat opposite with the document wallet on her knees and a board across the wallet. Beside her Bracken held the house's seal in its box on his lap, with both hands, as another man might have held a hat.

At the foot of the road the two coats stood under the chandler's eaves. They looked at the carriage, and at the coach, and at the wallet on Karis's knees through the wet glass. One of them took out a pencil.

"Sixth sitting," said Bracken, to nobody. "I said eleven. I should like it noted that I now say fourteen, and that I said so in the second month, and was thought gloomy."

"Nobody thought you gloomy," said Karis, without looking up from the board.

"The senior clerk did. He has since apologised."

The bridge was open, the river running brown and quick a long way under its second ring, and they went up the market street at a walk. There were perhaps a dozen people on the four steps of the wool exchange this morning, where once there had been thirty, and only two of them had folding boards. A merits sitting, the house's counsel had said, was a thing correspondents came to on the first day and the last. In between, it was paper, and paper did not sell.

The coachman took them round by the stable arch, as he always did, and set the brake under the wall.

The young officer at the side door had got over his cold at last, and he had the log open on its board, and the first name already written, in the hand of a man who had written it so often that he no longer saw it. Cael read it upside down as he went past, as he had read it at every sitting since the fortieth day. *Caelen Hesk-ward.* And in the second column, under *standing*: *the enrollee of record, unclassified.*

The officer wrote Karis's line under it without being told how. *Advocate of record for the respondent, under the hearing provision.* She had been in his book under that title since the fourth sitting, and he had stopped looking up to check the spelling.

The house's counsel was waiting inside, small and grey and straight-shouldered, with her bag in one hand and her steel rule in the other.

"He's going to give things away this morning," she said, by way of greeting. "I had it from his clerk's face at the inn. Don't thank him for any of it." She looked at Cael, from his boots up. "And you. Whatever he gives, you look as if you'd expected it since breakfast."

"I have," said Cael.

She considered that for a moment, and something that was not quite a smile came and went at the corner of her mouth.

"Yes," she said. "I suppose you have." And she led them into the long room.

---

Nothing in it had changed. He had half thought something would, after the recess, after Ostrand and the borrowed office four streets back, as he had half thought the hill would look different on the morning after the case.
