But the wool exchange smelled of lanolin and damp coats, as it always had. The forty benches ran back to the doors, a quarter full. Lot seventeen was cut into the floor under his left boot. On the platform the hood sat in its place behind the long table, grey and still, and below it the five chairs of the evaluation seat stood in their row with their covers off and five people in them. Four were district evaluators whose names he had heard read once and had written down; and in the middle, with a file open on her knee at its usual angle, sat Ilsev.

She did not look up. She was reading.

At the records table the senior chair had the minute, and Havel had the times and the receipts. Havel did not look up either; Cael had learned by now that the man would not, and no longer waited for it.

He sat, and put his hands flat on the house's table, one on each side of the place where his notebook would have gone if he had brought it into this room, which he never had.

He knew what was in the case on Karis's shelf, and that this room ran on it. The frame they meant to stand him in was the thing on the leaf, appended to the reading and not the reading, and every person on that platform would go home tonight not knowing it, and so would Jent, perhaps, and so would the clerk. He was the only one in the long room, besides Karis at his elbow, who knew what the argument was about.

His face did nothing. He had practised that at a counter, in a queue, behind a girl with a new badge.

"In the matter of Caelen Hesk-ward," said the seat's clerk. "Upon the merits. The sixth sitting."

Jent stood.

He stood as he always did, without hurry, with his spectacles hanging on their black cord and his empty hands at his sides, a man of middle height in a brown coat whose cuffs had been mended in a slightly different brown. His travelling inkwell sat on the petitioner's table with its lid screwed down. There was a single folder beside it, and he did not open the folder.

"If the seat pleases," he said, in his pleasant carrying voice, "the petitioner would save it some time."

The hood did not move.

"The respondent filed, in the week before the recess, a supplemental table of the renewals of this enrollment, with the day of each sitting and the day of each seal set side by side. I have read it. I read it twice, on a boat." He turned his head a little toward the house's table, toward Karis, and inclined it. "It is correct. The petitioner accepts it as filed, in every line, and adopts it."

At Cael's left the counsel's pen moved. She had her privileged minute open in front of her, and across the top of the page, in her small capitals, written before any of them sat down, stood three headings. *RENEWALS. NINE DAYS. EVALUATOR.* She drew a line through the first.

"It follows," said Jent, "that the second schedule to the petition is wrong in one particular. It suggests, at its ninth line, that the first renewal was sealed out of its order. It was not. It was sealed nine days after the panel sat, because an officer of an inspecting delegation held the registry's copy for his countersignature, as he was entitled to do, and the respondent's advocate has shown the seat the very leaf." He took one sheet from the folder after all, and held it out to the clerk. "The petitioner withdraws that line, and tenders the schedule again without it. I should not like the seat to rule upon a schedule with a mistake in it. Least of all one of mine."

There was a small sound along the front benches. The counsel drew her second line.

The clerk carried the sheet to the records table, and Havel took it, and laid it beside another leaf from his own pile, and bent over the two of them.

"And lastly," said Jent. "A great deal was said at the preliminary about the evaluator of record at Halcenvane. I said some of it. I should like it in this minute, in plain words, that the petitioner accepts that officer as competent in every particular, accepts his panels as properly held, and accepts every figure he has signed as honestly come by." He let his hands fall. "The petition relies upon none of these matters. It never has. I mention them only so that nobody is put to the trouble of proving what I do not dispute."

The third line. The counsel turned her steel rule a quarter turn on the table, and it ticked.

Three points, in something under ten minutes. Cael sat with his hands flat and counted them, because he could not help it, and waited to feel what he had felt at the preliminary when the first three headings went down, the hard small lift under the ribs of a thing won.

It did not come.

He looked for it, quite honestly. They had spent four days on that table of renewals, in the annex, at the start of the year. He had found the nine days himself, in his own old Log, and Karis had let him find them. He had been proud of it for a week. And a man in a mended coat had just stood up before a hood and said that every line of it was right, and adopted it, and thrown away a piece of his own petition rather than leave an error standing.

It was a clean win. It was as clean as anything in the whole proceeding. And it weighed nothing, because Jent had not needed the ground he was giving. He was a man clearing a table before a meal. Every concession made the petition smaller and harder, until there would be nothing left of it but the single thing it had always been: a question for the seat's discretion, about a frame.

*None, Advocate.* He had said so in the fourth sitting, in so many words, and Cael had written it down and had gone on bringing receipts.

Winning had become a form. That was the size of it. They filed, and it was received, and it was true, and it was conceded; and each time, the corridor was one door shorter and no wider.

"The supplemental submission of the respondent upon the renewals," said the clerk. "Eleven leaves. Adopted by the petitioner. Received and entered."

Then the hood spoke.

It did not speak often, and when it did the long room went still to hear it, even now, even on a day of paper. It had the same unhurried voice it had used at the preliminary, a voice with no grade in it.

"The seat notes the petitioner's acceptance of the evaluator of record. It will nonetheless have his notes before it. The respondent's house is ordered to produce that officer's notes upon the enrollee for the current academic year, entire, at the eighth sitting. So ordered."

"So ordered," said the clerk, and wrote.

Karis had it down before he had finished. Cael saw her write *Gault. Notes, entire. Eighth.* on the board in her wallet, and under it, after a second, *ordinary*.

It was. A production order was the most ordinary paper a seat could make. Bracken would carry it up the hill tonight in his coat, and Gault would read it in his office behind wing three with his instrument case very straight on the bench beside him, and would say that he had written it down. There was nothing in Gault's notes that could hurt anybody. There was a year of figures in them that breathed and never settled and never went past a red line.

The seat rose.

---

Jent overtook them on the steps.

The rain had thinned to a mist, and the market street ran shining down toward the river with the carts standing in it. Withrow had gone ahead to her carriage and bowed to nobody. Bracken was at the coach door with the seal. Jent came down the four steps behind the house's party with his one folder under his arm, and at the bottom he stopped beside Karis and waited until she had to look at him.

"Advocate," he said. "Your table was better than ours. I said so in there, but a minute is a dry place to say a thing. I wanted to say it on a step."

Karis had the wallet against her chest.

"You adopted it," she said. "You didn't have to."

"No. I could have let you prove it, line by line, across two sittings. You would have. It would have been a pleasure to watch." He put his spectacles on to look at the sky, and took them off again. "But it would still have been right at the end of it, and I'd have known that from the start. I dislike sitting through an argument whose answer I already hold. It seems a discourtesy to the person making it."

He nodded to her, and to Cael, with exactly the same nod, and went away down the market street with his folder under his arm. He did not look like a man who had given up three points.

Cael did not send the read after him. He had sent it five times and found nobody in the coat.

In the coach, going up, Karis sat with the wallet shut on her knees and looked out at the rain for the length of the bridge.

"We won three," she said.

"I know."

"Say it as if it mattered." She did not turn her head. "You said you'd come to every one. I said I'd write it as if it were ground. I can't do my half if you sit there looking the way you looked when the hood rose." Her pale fingers tightened on the wallet. "I saw you. You looked as if somebody had handed you a receipt for something you never bought."

He thought about that. It was fair; it was, in fact, exactly what it had felt like.

"We won three," he said, and made himself hear it as he said it. The nine days. Four days in the annex, and his own old Log, and her pencil stopping when he found it. "The table's in the minute now as the petitioner's own. Nobody can ever go behind it. Whatever they rule, they rule knowing every renewal was good."

"Yes," said Karis.

"That's not nothing."

"No." She let out a breath. "It isn't. Thank you." And she opened the wallet again, on her knees, and began to draft the covering leaf for Gault's notes while the coach climbed.

Bracken, in the corner, had shut his eyes. He opened one of them.

"Fourteen," he said.

---

Havel had the two leaves under his hands for a little less than four minutes.

It was not long, but it was longer than he had held any single thing at that table since the year began, and he was aware of every one of the minutes, because he was the man who wrote down the times.

The work was simple: a party had withdrawn a line from a schedule and tendered the schedule again without it. The junior chair's part was to lay the new leaf beside the old and see that nothing had changed between them but the line withdrawn; to receipt the new one, and mark the old one *superseded* in the corner, with the day and the sitting; and to pass both to the senior chair for the minute. It was in the second manual, at a page he could have found with his eyes shut.

He laid them side by side on the blotter and went down them with the point of a dry pen, a line at a time, as the manual said.

It was the petition's second schedule. He had receipted it once already, on the fortieth day, in a stack of nineteen leaves, and had not read it then, because a junior chair receipted leaves and did not read them. Today he had to read it. That was the whole of the task. The schedule was drawn in the old grammar that declarations used, a list of what the registry held to be so about the person the petition named, one thing to a line.

The ninth line was gone from the new leaf, as tendered. He checked the eighth against the eighth and the tenth against what was now the ninth.

The fourteenth line, on both leaves, was a designation with its title written out in full.

He knew the title. He had first seen its mark on a pump step, in a town a long way west of here, beside a form of four questions, when the young man at the far table had still been a boy. He had asked about it then, in the proper way, and had been told once, in a room with the door shut, that it was not his clearance. He had written that down, on a sheet of his own, and it lay in his kit tonight between the second manual and the third with five others.

The point of the dry pen rested under the fourteenth line on the old leaf, and then under the same line on the new. The words were the same. The spelling was the same. A capital that the copyist had made a little too tall on the first was a little too tall on the second, because the second had been copied from it by a careful hand.

That was what he was asked. Whether the two agreed. They agreed.

He moved the pen down to the fifteenth.

He did not look across the room. He had looked at that boy before, on the pump step and at Greyvane and on the sprung oak at the bluff, and he remembered every one of the looks, and he would not give himself another. He had decided that long ago, and the deciding had held through eleven weeks of Fifth-days in the same long room, forty feet from the house's table. It held this morning. He went down both leaves to the foot and found them alike in everything but the one line. Then he wrote *superseded* in the corner of the first, in the plain hand he kept for corners, with *sixth sitting* under it, and entered the second in the receipt book against the time.

His right hand ached at the web of the thumb. He opened and closed it once, under the table.

"Junior," said the senior chair, without turning his head, and Havel passed the leaves across.

The petitioner was speaking again by then, about an evaluator. Havel wrote the time.

---

He logged a great deal that fortnight.

The merits were heavier than the first sittings had been. There was not more talk; there was less. But every sitting put paper on his table, and all of it had to be receipted and numbered and laid against the lists. At the sixth sitting, the respondent's supplemental upon the renewals: eleven leaves, a table, a certified copy of a minute. At the seventh, a week later, the petitioner's acceptance of the tournament office's record, in one leaf; the respondent's second supplemental, upon the manner in which that record had been kept, in nineteen; and a correction slip, signed by the registrar of the house, altering the date on one exhibit by a single day, with the reason given.

He liked the correction slip, and was a little ashamed of how much. It was a small square of the house's paper, and it said that the registrar had found, on reading his own exhibit list a fourth time, that he had written the fifth of a month for the sixth, and begged leave to amend. Nobody would ever have caught it, and it could not have mattered to anything. The man had found it himself, and walked it down a hill.

Havel numbered it and pasted it in beside the line it corrected, and squared it with his thumbnail.

The house's exhibits stood at a hundred and seventy-nine by the end of the seventh sitting. He had receipted every one, and in eleven weeks he had not found among them a leaf out of order, a seal smudged past reading, or a count that did not come to what its contents leaf said it came to. He had sat junior to a good many proceedings. He had never sat junior to a record like it.

He thought the petitioner knew that. The petitioner said so, at every sitting, to the hood, in a voice that seemed to mean it, and then conceded another piece of ground; and the proceeding went on at the same pace as before, toward the same question. Havel wrote the times. It was not his place to notice where a proceeding was going. It was his place to see that when it got there, every leaf it had walked over could be found again.

At the inn by the bridge, on the night of the seventh sitting, he ate his supper in the long room downstairs among the carters, because the fire was better there, and then went up.

The river was quiet, for it had dropped since the flood weeks, and from his window the middle pier stood dry to its second ring, and the bridge-ward's lantern showed a plain black water going by under it with hardly a sound. He lit his own lamp and set the kit on the table and unbuckled it.

He took out the six sheets.

He had not had them out since the night he wrote the last. He laid them down in their order, left to right, and read them through twice, slowly, as the manual told a records officer to read anything he meant to rely on.

A mark in a boy's file with no office behind it. That was the first, and the oldest, and the hand it was written in was younger than his hand was now. Three more lay between, each in its year. The fifth was a sentence on a senior desk at Norhold, read upside down, shutting a door the first had found open. The sixth was a seal that stood in no registry of seals.

Six things the registers could not answer. He had written each of them down on the day he found it, as it had happened, and nothing of what he made of it.

He sat and looked at them.

He had thought, this morning a week ago, with the dry pen resting under a fourteenth line, that there might be a seventh. He could admit that here. For the space of perhaps a breath it had seemed to him that he was looking at something: the first sheet's mark, written out at last in a clerk's fair copy, in a schedule, on his own blotter. For four years he had known it only as a mark. Now it had been given a line and a number in a paper before a seat.

But that was not a gap. He had gone over it every night since, and it would not come out as one. The mark was in the schedule because the petition had put it there, openly, under seal, and the seat had received it. It was the first thing in four years that anybody had done with that mark in the proper way. The registers answered it perfectly. Second schedule, fourteenth line, received on the fortieth day, re-tendered on the hundred and seventeenth, both leaves alike.

There was nothing there to write. The six sheets held what the registers could not answer. This, they could.

And a sheet for what he felt, sitting forty feet from that table every Fifth-day with a pen in his hand, was a thing he had never kept and did not know how to begin.

He did not take a clean sheet from the kit. He looked at the place in it where the clean sheets were, and he left them there.

He thought instead, for a while, of the words on his rotation order. *The seat's Fifth-days, until relieved.* He had read them in the first week as he read any order, for what they required of him. He read them differently tonight, in his head, with the river going by. A man could be relieved because the work was done. He could also ask.

He had never asked to be moved off anything in his life. He did not ask now. He only noticed, as he might have noticed a door in a wall he had walked past for years, that the order had the word in it.

He put the six back between the second manual and the third, the same way round, and buckled the kit.

Then he sat a little longer at the window with his aching hand open on his knee, and watched the bridge-ward's lantern, until it went out.

---

Withrow sent for him on the Second-day evening, by the porter's boy, and when he came to her door she was writing.

She did not stop, but pointed with her left hand at the chair across the desk, and he sat in it, and she went on to the end of her line and the end of her leaf. She signed it with her whole name, and then she blotted it and laid it face down on a pile at her right hand.

There was a pile at her left hand too. It was taller.

"Thirty-four," said Withrow. "You like counting, so I'll give you the number before you start on it yourself. Thirty-four letters from the heads of other chartered houses, since the preliminary. They have come in ones and twos. Bracken puts them on my desk in the morning with the seals uppermost, so that I can see whose they are before I decide whether to have breakfast."

She was a tall woman, and she sat very upright, and tonight for the first time since he had known her she looked as if she had been sitting upright for a long while. The lamp was turned low. The charter hung in its frame on the wall behind her with its locked case beneath.

"Eleven of them are kind," she said. "I answer those last, because they're easy and I save them. Nineteen want to know what the house will do if the seat goes against it. That is a fair question from people whose own charters are written in the same words as mine." She touched the taller pile. "And four have advised me, very civilly, to let you go."

Cael did not say anything.

"Not expel. Nobody has written that word. *Release.* *Come to an accommodation.* One of them wants me to *spare the house a ruling*." Her voice was quite even. "Their reasoning is not foolish. If this seat rules that those sections can be turned upon a person for what he is, that ruling stands in the registry's books for every house on the continent. If I let you go before it rules, there is no ruling. One boy leaves one hill, and thirty-four charters sleep sound. The man who put it most plainly has been my friend for twenty years. He wrote it in his own hand, and it cost him something to write."

"What did you answer?"

"That the thing he's afraid of is done the day a house hands over an enrollee to avoid it. After that, nobody needs a ruling. They need only file, and wait for us to count the cost." She squared the face-down pile with the side of her hand. "I put it more gently than that. He's a good man, and frightened, and I have been both."

The fire ticked. He looked at the two piles, and at her right hand, which had ink on the second finger to the knuckle.

"You're answering them yourself," he said. "All of them."

"Every one."

"Bracken has clerks."

"He has. Excellent ones, and they've offered." She capped the inkwell. "A clerk's answer is the house's answer. These people did not write to the house. They wrote to a woman they've sat beside at convenings since before you were enrolled anywhere, to ask whether she has lost her judgment. That is not a question I can have answered for me in a good round hand. They would know it for what it was."

She looked at him then, directly, as she had looked at him across this desk with a charter folder open between them.

"I didn't send for you to be thanked, and I'll be annoyed if you try. I sent for you because you keep a column. I've seen it. Four names at the head." She did not ask how she had seen it, and he did not ask either. "I would rather you had the right figure under mine than a guess. Thirty-four letters, and thirty-four answers by the end of the week, and not one of them says I'm sorry I did it."

He found he could not speak for a moment.

He knew a thing she did not. He knew that there was a door, one day wide, that would let him walk out of her house after a ruling and leave nothing in her folder but an entry in an old enrollment book. He knew that he was sitting through every Fifth-day in a room he no longer believed in, in part so that the door would still be there when it was wanted, and her charter clean on the other side of it. He could not tell her any of that. He could not tell her why he had stopped believing in the room.

She was answering thirty-four letters by hand for a boy who was keeping the largest thing he knew from her. It was not a lie. It sat in the room exactly like one.

"I'll write it down right," he said.

"I know you will. That's why I told you." She drew the next letter from the taller pile and turned it to the lamp, and he saw the seal on it, blue-grey, the lake house's. "Go to bed. You've a floor the day after tomorrow, and I'm told you're to look ordinary on it."

At the door he turned.

"Chancellor. The four. Did any of them say what they'd have done? In your chair, on the eighth day?"

Withrow did not look up from the lake house's letter.

"No," she said. "It's the one thing none of the four thought to put in."

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-06-the-compacts-hand/manuscript/chapter-45.md

# Chapter 45 — The Lake House

Bracken brought the schedule to the old frames himself, on the Sixth-day, in the hour after the noon meal when he knew Brom would be there.

He came in by the yard door with a single sheet in his hand and his spectacles pushed up on his forehead, and stood a moment inside it while his eyes found the three of them: Ephram on the long frame, in the middle of a set; Brom beside the short one with his arms folded; Cael against the wall. The old frames stood in a low stone room behind the stables that had been a harness store once and still smelled of it, with two lamps, a sanded floor, and the two great timber frames with their iron bars and springs, older than anybody on the hill.

"From the district seat," said Bracken. "By this morning's bag. It is addressed to the practitioner, care of the registrar, and so I have read it, and so I have walked it across a wet yard, which I do not do for everything." He held it out. "It is the review's schedule of evidence."

Brom unfolded his arms and took it, and read it standing, without moving his lips, and then he held it a little away from him and read it aloud, slowly, the way he read anything official, as if testing each plank before he put his weight on it.

"*Ostrand district registry. Review for advancement of tier: Brom, enrolled at Halcenvane. Schedule of evidence. The panel will read, first, the practitioner's record as entered, the continental title included. Second, the house's attendance and floor sheets for the two years preceding, to be supplied by the registrar under the house's seal. Third, a reading in the sigil frame, under load, taken on the day in the presence of the three assessors. No testimony is contemplated. Sitting: the last Fifth-day of the academic year, at the second bell.*"

He lowered it.

"The second is mine," said Bracken. "I have supplied it. It went down this morning by the return bag and weighed four pounds nine ounces, and there is not a blot in it." He pulled his spectacles down onto his nose. "The first is the registry's own. The third is yours, and nobody can supply it for you." He looked at the short frame, and then at Brom's left shoulder, briefly, as he might have glanced at a column he had already added. "I take it that is what this room is for."

"It's what it's been for all year," said Brom.

"Then I shall leave you to it." Bracken turned to go, and turned back, as he always did. "Under load," he said. "I asked the district clerk what load. He wrote back that it is the assessors' to set on the day, and that in his experience they set it at what the title was won against, and a little over." He nodded once to each of them, the small nod, and went out into the yard.

Nobody said anything until the door had shut.

Then Ephram, who had not stopped his set, finished it, and let the long frame's bar come back to its rest with both hands, and went to the rack by the wall where the short frame's iron plates hung on their pegs. He took down two, and looked at them, and hung one back, and took down a heavier one in its place.

He did not ask what the title had been won against. A man who had kept two years of somebody else's figures on three sheets of ruled paper was not likely to have missed Brom's.

He slid the plates onto the short frame's bar, one each side, and stepped back, and looked at Brom.

Brom folded the schedule along its folds and put it into his left inside pocket, with the summons and the docket and the confirmation, and his hand stayed there a moment, flat on the cloth, over the square shape of the thing that was also in that pocket and had been since the forty-fifth day. Then he took the hand away and walked in under the bar.

---

Cael had watched Brom hold things for years. He had never before watched him practise it.

The short frame was a simple machine: two timber uprights, a crossbar of iron that ran in greased slots between them, and under each end of the bar a spring as thick as a man's wrist. You stood beneath the bar and took it on your shoulders, or on your forearms crossed above your head, and somebody let the catches go, and the springs drove it down. A sigil frame at a district seat was a finer thing, with a plate that read what the practitioner's Path did under the weight. This one read nothing. It only pressed.

Brom set his feet and crossed his forearms over his head, the right over the left, and Cael saw the skin change.

It always began at the wrists. It was not a colour, exactly, though the lamp found a dull grey in it, like a blade that has been oiled and put away. It was more that the surface of him stopped behaving like skin, and stopped creasing. It went up his forearms and across his shoulders under the shirt and down into his back, and where it had gone, Brom looked less like a man holding still and more like something that had been built there.

Ephram let the catches go.

The bar came down on Brom's crossed forearms with a sound like an anvil taking a hammer. The springs sang, and Brom's knees gave an inch and stopped.

Cael let the old read go out across the sanded floor.

It was Brom's own gift he read him with, taken years ago and used every day since, and it came back to him as it always did, as weight and where the weight went. The bar's load ran down through the crossed forearms and into the shoulders, the right taking a little more than the left, and from there down the long muscles of the back into the hips and through both heels into the floor. It was like watching water find its way down a roof. Nothing in the whole of Brom was pushing back against the bar; he was only giving the weight somewhere to go.

And the left shoulder was taking a little less than its share, though not by much. Brom knew; Cael could read him knowing, in the small care with which the left elbow was set.

Ephram counted under his breath, and at thirty he threw the catches, and the bar went up, and Brom stepped out from under it and let his arms down, and the skin went back to being skin, wrists last.

He stood breathing.

Ephram took a third plate off the rack, a small one, and held it up, and raised his eyebrows.

"Left side," said Brom.

Ephram hung it on the left.

They did it eleven times, by Cael's count, in the better part of an hour, and in all that time neither of them said another word that was not a number or a side. Ephram put the plates on and took them off, and watched Brom's left elbow the whole while with the level, patient attention he gave everything he meant to be right about. Twice he shook his head at nothing Cael could see, and took a plate off, and Brom did not argue. Once he added one that Brom had not asked for, and Brom looked at it and walked in under the bar.

On the eleventh the left shoulder took its whole share. Cael felt it through the floor, the weight going down both sides alike, and held, and held, to a count of forty.

The bar went up.

"The left's back," said Ephram.

It was the first whole sentence he had spoken since Bracken left, and he wiped his hands on the cloth at his belt and began taking the plates off the bar, one at a time, and hanging them on their pegs in order of weight.

Brom sat down on the bench by the wall, heavily, with his forearms on his knees. There were two long red bars across them where the iron had lain, and he looked at them for a while.

"They'll find it," he said. He did not seem to be speaking to either of them in particular. "That's the thing. I've known since the docket came that they'd find it, and I know now what the paper is that they'll write it on. I told you in the reading room. I can't read that sheet anymore." He turned his forearms over. "And I still want to stand in their frame and have the plate say what's true. I've been thinking about why. I think it's only that I'd like to have been right about myself, once, in front of somebody whose job it was to check."

"Lira said something like that," said Cael.

"Did she?" Brom thought about it. "She would."

He did not say what he would do with the finding afterward, but his hand went to the pocket again and rested there. Ephram, at the rack, with his back to them, hung up the last plate and squared it on its peg, and if he saw the hand he gave no sign of it.

---

The lake house came up the bluff road on the Second-day in a hired wagon with a blue-grey cloth over its hoops, and half the hill was at the north wall to watch it climb.

There were five of them: an instructor in a long coat, a clerk with a board, two fighters who had come to watch and carry, and the Blade. He got down in the outer court and stood looking about him at the lodge and the arch and the covered walk with frank interest, a square, fair, good-humoured young man of twenty or so with a wooden lath in a canvas sleeve across his back. He was Iron Rank Five, and he had beaten the Current girl on figures at the confluence, after four level exchanges, and had come and found her afterward to tell her that he had not enjoyed a bout so much all year. She had told Lira, and Lira had said that it was a very annoying thing for him to have done.

It was the last of the three bouts Rooke had struck, and the one she had wanted most.

They fought it on the Crown yard's sprung oak in the afternoon, under a white sky, with the house on the tiers. Rooke had chalked it on the yard's board himself. *Exhibition. Wind, Iron One, Halcenvane. Blade, Iron Five, the lake house. Five exchanges at most. Two touches ends it.* Three cards at the table: the lake house's instructor, the Mire instructor from the wing, and the Stone, who sat between them with his big hands folded and looked as if he had been carved there.

Cael had the east tier, with Brom on one side of him and Karis on the other. Karis had brought no notebook; she had said on the stair that she was going to watch one thing this year with her hands empty, and she sat on them to be sure.

"She's nervous," said Brom.

"She's not," said Cael. He was watching Lira's feet at the south chalk. "She's bored of being nervous. Look."

She was standing with her weight on the right.

Not evenly. On the right, plainly, the left foot only resting, the way anybody stands who has a favourite leg. She had been landing on that foot for seventeen days, in the east hall, at dawn with the program and at night without it. He had not been asked to the nights, and had only seen the stockings she threw away.

The steward's hand came down.

A Blade did not fight with his hands. He had the lath, three feet of pale ash with a bound grip, and his Path put an edge on it that was not the wood's, and a line out beyond its tip that he could read the way Cael read a floor. He committed to a cut in three beats: the heel, the hip, the edge. Once he had begun the first, the third came, and he could not take it back; that was a Blade's strength, and it was the door in him.

He began with a commitment at once, a long clean diagonal at shoulder height, to see what she would do.

She lied to it. Front foot. Back foot. He was already in his second beat and could not answer either. On his third, as the lath came through, she went, burst one, low and inside the cut, along the line of his own arm, and the end of that line was a place on the oak just behind his right hip.

Her right foot came down on it.

It came down whole. Cael saw the knee take the weight and the oak give under her and come back, the sprung floor's own gift, and she rose off it with the beat exactly where it belonged. Her palm was flat on the Blade's ribs before his lath had finished travelling through the place where she had been.

"Touch," said the steward. "Halcenvane."

The tiers made a noise, and somewhere along the west side the Current girl's voice went up over everybody's in a shriek. Brom let his breath out through his nose.

"On the right," said Karis, very quietly, to her knees.

"On the right," said Cael.

---

The Blade laughed.

He stood at his chalk with the lath across his shoulders and laughed, out loud, shaking his head, exactly as the Force had laughed at her on the painted floor at the start of the year. Then he stopped laughing and looked at the oak for a moment, at the place behind his own hip, and Cael saw him put it away somewhere to be used.

He did not commit in the second.

He stood, with the lath hanging in his right hand and its tip a few inches off the floor, and let her run the whole count at him, three lies and the truth, and did not move for any of it. He was reading. A Blade's line ran out past the end of his edge, and where it ran he knew what would cross it, a breath before it crossed.

She went on the fourth, because the count said to. Burst two.

He had the line laid already. He did not cut at her; he simply turned his wrist, and the lath came up and lay along the path she was travelling, like a bar dropped across a doorway, and she arrived on it. It took her on the forearm, the left, the one she carried close.

"Touch. The lake house."

One each. The tiers groaned, cheerfully. The Blade lifted the lath to her in a small salute, and Lira, walking back, rubbed her forearm and gave him a look that made the lake house's two carriers nudge one another.

The third was nobody's. They had each found the other's way in, and neither would walk through the same door twice. She lied and did not go; he began a commitment and she stepped out of its line before the hip, so that the edge came through empty air a yard from her. She walked him round the oak, and he turned with her, the lath low, laying lines and lifting them. Twice she might have gone and did not. She had one burst left under Rooke's rule and she was keeping it in her pocket as she had kept one in the east hall, seventeen days ago, and lost.

"Time," said the steward. "No touch."

At the table Rooke had not written anything for some while, and had his pencil behind his ear and his arms folded on the board.

The fourth.

The Blade had seen her land twice now, both times on the right. Cael could read it in the man's weight from the tier: a little turn of the front foot, a line laid early, not along her path this time but at its end. He was going to do what the Current girl had done. He was going to go and stand where her foot came down.

She ran the count. Front. Back. Shoulders. On the fourth she went, her last, along the inside of a cut he had not made, to a place on the oak that both of them could see, and her right foot reached for it.

The lath was there. It came across level, a hand above the boards, at the place where her right ankle would be when the weight went on.

She did not put the weight on.

Cael saw it and for a breath his stomach turned over, because it looked like the old thing, the touch and the step. It was not the old thing. Her right foot touched the oak and left it again, on purpose, lightly, like a hand taken off a hot kettle, and she was over the lath and down on the left, a full pace past him, with the beat under her and her back to his back.

She had not asked the foot. She had chosen.

She turned on the left and put her hand between his shoulder blades.

"Touch," said the steward. "Halcenvane. Two touches to one."

The house stood up.

It stood up all at once, three tiers of it, with a roar that sent the pigeons off the lecture range. Brom was on his feet with both fists over his head, the left as high as the right. Karis had taken her hands out from under her and was clapping them, which Cael had not seen her do since Greyvane. Down on the oak the Blade had turned round and was holding his lath out to Lira across both palms, grinning all over his face, and she was pushing it back at him, and the Current girl had come over the rail and was running at them both.

Cael stayed sitting a moment longer than the rest.

He had seen the foot touch and lift, where everybody else had seen a girl jump a stick. He looked across the yard at the table, and Rooke was looking at the same place on the oak, with his pencil in his hand at last. He wrote one word on the card. Then, which Cael had never seen him do, he wrote a second.

The panel's figures went up on the yard's board in the porter's boy's chalk. *Wind, 28. Blade, 26.* She did not look at them until the Current girl turned her round by the shoulders and made her.

---

The slate outside the east hall had a new line on it the next morning.

Rooke had put it at the top, above the week's drills, and ruled a chalk line under it, so that nobody could take it for anything that would be wiped off on the Seventh-day.

*Advancement evaluation, Silver bracket. The district seat, Ostrand. The first sitting after the year's close. Program's entrant: the Wind.*

Lira read it with her wraps in her hand, for she had come down early, and so had Cael, and the covered walk was empty but for the two of them and Rooke, who was standing a little way off with his board, looking out at the rain as though he had nothing to do with the slate.

She read it twice. Her face did not change the second time, and that was how Cael knew.

"That's after the ruling," she said.

She said it to the slate, in an ordinary voice, and it was not a question. She had a calendar in her head as good as Bracken's, and she had laid two dates on it and seen which came first.

"It is," said Rooke.

He did not turn round, but went on looking at the rain. Cael watched the back of his head and could not have said what the man knew. Rooke had eaten in the open seat at a refectory table on the day of the riders, and had struck three bouts off a card without asking how an ankle had come to be turned on the way to a sitting. He knew what a ruling could do to an enrollment, and he knew whose friend his entrant was. He had built her a hall. He had never once asked her for a promise.

Nobody said anything for the space of perhaps five breaths.

"Then I'd better be ready for it," said Lira.

"You'd better." Rooke turned from the rain at last, and looked at her, and then for a moment past her at Cael, with a face that gave away exactly as much as his board did. "Twenty-eight is a figure. It isn't the figure. Wraps. Every count ends on whichever foot I call, and I'll call it late."

She went in, and at the door she looked back at the slate once, quickly, at the ruled line, and Cael saw her decide not to say whatever it was.

---

She asked him that night, on the north wall, with the valley dark below and the coats' two lanterns small and yellow at the foot of the road.

"I want the key tonight," she said. "And this time I want you."

"The honest floor? Brom's—"

"Not the honest floor. Not Brom and not Karis. The two of us." She had her knees drawn up on the coping and her arms round them. "I want the rate you never show."

He looked at her.

"You've seen it. You were at Norhold. You've stood on the honest floor since the first week of the year and written every bill on your hand."

"I've seen pieces. I've seen what one lane costs, with Karis counting. I've seen you spend five pushes at a wall of Brom so that we could find out where your shoulder starts to talk." She shook her head. "That's a ledger. I've never once stood in front of all of it at the same time, with you trying to beat me. Nobody has. Daeva has, and she's Gold, and you were on one knee at the end of it." She put her chin on her knees. "I fought a Blade yesterday who's four ranks over me, and I knew what he was by the second exchange. I've known you since Ardenmere and I don't know what you are with the lid off. I'd like to. Once. Before."

She did not say before what, and he did not ask.

"It won't be pretty," he said.

"I didn't ask for pretty."

"I'll price it first. That's the rule, and it's initialled."

"Price it, then. But don't score it." She unfolded herself from the coping. "Tenth bell. Bring the book if you must. I'm not writing on my hand."

He priced it at his sill, by the lamp, in four lines. *Night, the east hall, L. alone. Everything public, at the rate, to win. Buys: she knows. Spends: hip past five on boards; shoulder; ten counts for a lane, twelve for a third; behind the eyes. Not the quiet thing. Not the new one.* He looked at *she knows* for a moment and left it.

The hall was cold, and the shutters were down, and she had lit both lanterns and chalked two marks on the painted floor thirty feet apart. She was at the south one in her wraps with her weight even, and he left his coat and the satchel on the bench and went to the north.

"No table," said Lira. "No count. We go till I say stop. If I touch you, I'll say so. You needn't."

"All right."

"Don't be kind."

He stood on his chalk and let go of the room he had been fighting inside all year.

That was what it felt like. He had lived inside the house's rate since Norhold as a man lives inside a coat, four on boards and never a fifth, two contacts, the give shut, the push a flat hand, the lane nowhere. He did not take the coat off; he let it fall, and stood there in the lantern light with all of it awake at once, and it was so much easier than holding it that his eyes stung.

She ran the count.

He did not wait for the fourth. The read had her weight before her front foot had finished its lie, deep, to the hips, and it said where the truth would come from a full beat before she spent it. He went first. Her own burst, the first thing he had ever taken, low and flat off the left foot, straight across the count; and he saw her eyes widen, because nobody had ever simply walked through it before. She burst to meet him, late. He was already down, and the landing beat gave him back, and he put the push into the boards at her landing place a hand's breadth before her foot arrived.

It was not a flat hand but the push at fighting load, the full shove of the thing, and the air over that square yard of floor went solid for an instant and then gave. Her foot came down on nothing where she had expected, and she sat down hard, with his palm already on her shoulder.

Three seconds, perhaps. He stepped back.

Lira sat on the painted floor with her mouth open.

"Again," she said.

She did not run the count the second time. She stayed back, out of the push's reach, moving on her toes along the far line of crosses, making him come to her, which was exactly right. He did not come; he asked the air to lean.

Fifteen feet of it, from his chalk to the space behind her left shoulder, a third deep. His ears closed and opened. The dust in the cracks of the boards woke and ran, pale, down the lane, and under the lanterns it threw a thin moving shadow, and he saw her see the shadow and know it. She had learned to read that band on this floor in the year's seventh week. She burst along its edge to get out from under where he would arrive.

He took the read off the lane and gave it to her, the way he had learned to, and stepped in.

It carried him a third faster than any feet. Her burst had been along the lane's edge; he came down the lane's middle and out of its end, past her, behind her, while her landing beat was still coming up through her knees, and touched her between the shoulder blades, where she had touched the Blade.

Then the bill came in.

The warm hand over the inside of his skull. Everything slow. He stood where he was and began the count, as he always did. *One. Two.*

She had turned. She was looking at his face.

*Three.*

"That's the ten," she said softly, and went.

She came straight at him, burst on burst, her second and her third with no count between them, and he called the Wind to go and it came a beat behind his asking, and he called the read and it came late, and he got his forearm up and opened the give and it opened *late*, and by then her palm was flat against his ribs, on the right side, exactly where they had been cracked.

"Touch," said Lira. "Mine."

*Eight. Nine.* He stood with her hand on his ribs and waited for ten, and at eleven the hand lifted off the inside of his head.

He was grinning; he could feel it, and he could not remember when he had last grinned on a floor.

"You went inside the count."

"I've written that count on my palm since the first week of the year. Did you think I'd never spend it?" She was grinning too, breathing hard. "Again. And don't lay a lane, or I'll do it twice."

After that he did not give her anything.

He let her strike, and she put three fast palms into his raised forearm, one, two, three, and the give took all three like a cup filling, and the fourth she did not throw, because she had counted that as well. He closed the distance on the third and touched her wrist, on the tape, with one bright contact of Karis's spark. She yelped and shook her hand, and by then his other palm was on her collarbone. He went past five on the boards and felt the hip begin to write its bill, crest to knee, and went on, a sixth and a seventh, and the push again, and the read never once off her.

She was very good, better than she had been yesterday in the Crown yard, and twice she found a line he had not shut and was a hand away from him.

It did not matter. He was not faster than her. He could see her understand that, somewhere in the eighth exchange or the ninth. He was only earlier. Everything she did, he had an answer for a half beat before she did it, and the answer came from a different drawer every time.

Once, when she planted both feet to change direction, the new thing lifted its head at them. *There?* He named them without breaking stride. *Lira's. Leave them.* It cost him the half beat, and she nearly had him a second time.

"Stop," said Lira.

He stopped.

She was bent over with her hands on her knees, and he found that he was not standing very well himself. His right hip had stopped writing and begun to shout, and his shoulder had counted the pushes and had a view about the last one, and there was a slow thick ache behind his eyes that was the lane and the naming together.

She straightened up, and looked at him for a long moment in the lantern light. She was not frightened, as he had been afraid, on the wall, that she would be. Her face was the one she had turned on the Blade's lath, held out to her across his palms: somebody had offered her a thing, and she was deciding whether it was hers to take.

"Right," she said. "Now I know."

"What do you know?"

"What it's going to look like." She sat down on the boards, all at once, with her legs out. "When somebody finally gets that in daylight, with a table and a yard full of people. I know what their face is going to do." She wiped her forehead with her wrist. "And I know you liked it."

He did not answer at once.

"Yes," he said.

"Good. You should. You've been walking round this hill for two years like a man carrying a full pail up a stair." She looked at the chalk mark he had left, thirty feet away. "I'm not going to tell anybody about tonight."

"Not Brom?"

"Not Brom. He'd want to know if you were all right, and you are, and I'd have to say it six times. Not Karis; she'd want it measured, and it isn't a figure. Not the captain, ever, though he'd give his chart to have stood where I'm sitting." She shook her head. "It isn't going on my hand and it isn't going in my book. Somebody ought to have seen it once who wasn't keeping score. That's all it was for. It's mine."

"The costs go in the Log. That's the rule."

"The costs. Hip, shoulder, eyes. Not the exchanges." She held out a hand, and he pulled her up, and his hip told him what it thought of that. "Not that I had one off you."

"You had one off me."

"I know I did," said Lira, with great satisfaction. "I just don't want it written down."

He wrote the bill at the bench, standing, because sitting had become a question. *Hip, crest to knee, from the sixth. Shoulder, three pushes at load. One lane: ten. A naming in the middle of it. Behind the eyes till morning, I'd guess.* He stopped with the pen lifted. Then he added, because the book was to be kept honest and this was a cost too: *It was easier than the band, by a long way. Mind that.*

She was at the great door when he had done, with her hand on its edge.

"*Door*," she said. "*It's held.*" And then, as he went through it: "You were early. Every time. I'm going to think about early for a month."

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-06-the-compacts-hand/manuscript/chapter-46.md

# Chapter 46 — The Last Line

Gault carried his own notes down the hill.

He would not let the house's escort have them, or Bracken, or the coach's boot. He rode inside, on the eighth sitting's morning, with the bound volume on his knees in the house's boards and both hands laid on it, and the case with the handle set very straight on the seat beside him, as if the instruments in it might be wanted to swear to something. He was a spare, exact man with a magister's grey at his temples, and Cael had sat across a trestle from him every week for two years and had never before seen him out of a room he was in charge of. He looked at the wet streets of Ostrand going by with a kind of mild disapproval, as a man looks at a column somebody else has added.

"I have been an evaluator of record eleven times," he said, to the coach in general, as they crossed the bridge. "I have never before been asked for the notes. The figures, yes. The minute, always. Not the notes." He considered it. "I find I do not mind. I should mind very much if I had written them differently for being read."

"Did you?" said Karis.

"I did not know they would be. That is the use of not knowing."

The young officer at the side door wrote him in the log under *standing* as *evaluator of record to the respondent's enrollment*, and Gault leaned over the board and read it upside down, and nodded.

The long room was a little fuller than it had been, for somebody had heard that an officer would be called. Lira and Brom were on the fourth bench, where the house sat when it came to watch, and Lira had her right foot tucked under the left, out of nothing but habit now. Up on the platform the hood was in its place, with Ilsev and her four below it; at the records table sat the senior chair and the junior, who did not look up.

"In the matter of Caelen Hesk-ward," said the seat's clerk. "Upon the merits. The eighth sitting. Production, under the seat's order of the sixth."

The house's counsel rose, and said that the house produced, and sat, which was all she was needed for.

"The evaluator of record," said the clerk.

Gault went up to the clerk's table with the volume, and laid it down, and squared it.

"Are these your notes upon the enrollee for the current academic year?"

"They are."

"Made by you, at the time?"

"Each before I left the room in which the thing was observed. Where I could not write at once, I have said so in the margin, and by how long."

"Entire?"

"Entire." Gault's hand was still on the board. "I was asked for the notes upon the enrollee. I have taken that to mean every note in which he appears, and not only the notes of his bouts. There are seventeen panel notes, one to a supervised session, with the three cards and the figure. There are also five notes that are not of any panel." He lifted the hand. "I mention it so that the seat is not surprised by the leaf for the year's seventy-sixth day."

Something moved under the hood. It was the smallest alteration, a stillness becoming a different stillness, and Cael, who had watched that grey shape for eight sittings, felt it in the back of his neck.

The seventy-sixth day. Four riders at the gate, and an order read aloud under the arch.

"The clerk will read the contents leaf," said the hood.

The clerk read it: seventeen sessions by date, each with its floor and its figure, and then the five. A note on the enrollee's excusal for injury, with the healer's line; two notes on the visitors' gallery; a note on the production of the wing's records to this seat. And the fifth, which the clerk read out in the same voice as the others, because it was written in the same hand.

"*Seventy-sixth day. An order was presented at the house's gate, by four riders, in the name of an office styled facilities, for the enrollee's attendance upon an assessment. No assessment of this enrollee has been notified to the evaluator of record, by any office, at any time. I have written that down.*"

The long room was very quiet.

Jent had risen, and stood with his hands at his sides and his spectacles hanging, and for the first time in eight sittings Cael thought he saw the man choose his words.

"The petitioner has no knowledge of the matter in that note," said Jent. "I say so for the minute, and I say it carefully. It is no part of the petition. No instrument of the kind described was sought by the petitioner or issued at his instance." He paused. "As to the rest of the volume: the petitioner accepts it, as he said he would. He relies on none of it."

"Received," said the hood. "Entire. And entered."

That was all. The clerk wrote, and at the records table the junior chair drew the volume toward him and opened his receipt book. In the middle chair below the platform, Ilsev had turned a leaf in her file and was writing something small at its foot, and she did not look at the house's table, and she did not look at the hood.

Cael sat with his hands flat on the wood.

A magister had just put into a registry seat's own minute, under its own order, in a clerk's level voice, that somebody had tried to take the respondent off his hill by a paper nobody would own. It had been received, and it would lie in the registry's presses beside the table of renewals for as long as there was a registry.

And nothing whatever would come of it; he knew that as surely as he knew the lot number under his boot. Nobody on that platform would ask whose office it was, because the district seat had already answered in two sentences that the office was duly constituted and the house was referred elsewhere. The note would be true, and entered, and it would change the ruling by not one word.

He looked along the house's table at Gault, who had come back and sat down and set his case straight. The magister's face was quite calm. He had been asked for his notes, and he had given them, all of them, the inconvenient with the rest. He had not expected it to do anything; it had simply not occurred to him to leave a leaf out.

*That is how the honest ones do it*, Cael thought. They did it not to win, but because the leaf was there.

"The second supplemental submission of the respondent," said the clerk, "upon the recorded invocations of the integrity provisions. Nineteen leaves and a table. Received and entered."

Karis ticked her card, and the counsel turned her rule a quarter turn.

On the steps afterward Gault stood a moment in the thin rain with the empty boards under his arm and looked up at the green cupola.

"They asked me whether my figures were mine," he said. "In front of forty benches."

"What did you make of it, Magister?" said Lira.

"I have written that down," said Gault, and went to find the coach.

---

He found Karis on the floor.

It was some days after the eighth sitting, on an afternoon of hard bright cold, and he had come up the four flights with a question about the ninth that he forgot at the door. The reading room's table stood bare to its cloth, with the chairs pushed in, and the lamp in the window was not lit, because it was day. And under the window, on the boards, with her back against the wall and her knees up and a square of planed deal across them, sat Karis, writing in pencil.

She had a lantern beside her, though the sun was on her hair, and three bundles in front of her on the floor, each wrapped in oilcloth and tied with cord, and a fourth open on its cloth with its leaves in a squared stack and a single sheet lying on top.

"Don't step on the cord," she said, without looking up.

He did not step on the cord, and sat down on the floor opposite her, since it seemed to be where people were sitting.

"What are you doing?"

"Finding out what I need a table for." She finished her line and blew the pencil dust off it. "I've had one since I was eleven. I've never asked what it does. So I've taken it away, to see what stops working." She held up her left hand and turned down fingers. "Ink stops working. You can't keep a well level on your knees, and I've spoiled a cuff finding that out, so it's pencil, and a knife for the pencil. The lamp stops working, because a lamp wants a flat place and a lantern only wants a hook. Laying six things side by side stops working. I can lay two." She looked at the bundles. "And the shelf stops working. That's the one I didn't expect."

"The shelf."

"I know where everything is in this room because of where it sits. Third shelf, grey box, behind the tab. That isn't knowing where it is. That's the shelf knowing." She tapped the single sheet on top of the open bundle. "So every bundle carries its own contents leaf now, on top, under the first fold of the cloth. What's in it, in order, with a line for each. If somebody handed me one of these in the dark in a field, I could find a leaf in it by counting."

She said *in a field* quite lightly, as she might have said *in a cellar* or *on the moon*.

"How long?" said Cael.

"To find a leaf? In the annex, off the shelf, eleven seconds. I had the assistant time me." She pulled a face. "Out of oilcloth, on my knees, the first day: four minutes and some. And I tore a corner. Yesterday, fifty seconds. I want it under thirty, and I want it under thirty with cold hands, so I've had the window open since noon."

He had wondered why the room was so cold.

He looked at the four bundles. They were the Greyvane notebooks, he thought, or part of them, and some of the year's papers from Norhold; he knew the grey of the boards showing at one bundle's edge. The oilcloth was new, and the cord was the brown waxed kind the post room used. She had tied each one with the same knot, a flat one that lay under the thumb, and he saw that she had tied and untied them often enough for the cord to have taken the shape.

The case stood on its shelf between the two grey boxes, where it had stood since the day it was opened. She had not wrapped that, and he did not ask.

"It's a habit," said Karis. She had followed his eyes, and she answered a different question from the one he had not asked. "A habit's a thing you build before you need it. That's all this is. I'd be a poor sort of archivist if I could only keep an archive in the one room where somebody had given me a key." She bent over the deal board again. "Ask me what you came up to ask me."

"I've forgotten it."

"Then hold this cord."

He held the cord while she folded the oilcloth over the fourth bundle, corner and corner and the long flap last, and then she took the cord from him and tied her flat knot, and looked at Hesk's clock, which he had not noticed she had borrowed from his sill.

"Forty-four," she said. "It loses, doesn't it? Call it forty-six."

---

The assistant found out the next morning, and Cael was there for that too, because Karis had asked him to carry the code's second volume across the landing.

The girl was at her table in the old lumber room, where she sat every morning from the first bell, with ink on her collar and yesterday's digest squared in front of her. She was a second-year, and she had come to that table at the start of the year unable to find a section without being told its number, and she could now find one from a struck heading in an index a century old, and Cael did not think she had noticed the difference.

Karis laid a sheet on the table in front of her.

It was the girl's own work, a morning's abstract of some old ruling, a page and a half in a round careful hand. She looked at it as she looked at one every morning: quickly, all over, for the pencil mark. Every morning since the year began there had been one, never more than one, a small tick in the margin beside the line that was wrong, and the two words *find it*. It was the rule of the room: one mistake handed back, so that she would have to go and hunt the rest herself.

She turned the sheet over. She turned it back.

"There isn't one," she said.

"No," said Karis.

"You've forgotten to mark it."

"I haven't."

"Then you've—" The girl stopped. She looked at the page again, properly this time, a line at a time, with her lips pressed together. "Did you check it?"

"Twice. Then I was cross, and checked it a third time against the roll, in case you'd been clever." Karis had her hands behind her back. "You hadn't been clever. You'd been right. All the way down."

The assistant went red from her inky collar to her hair, and did not seem to know what to do with her hands, and in the end she put them flat on the sheet, one on each side, as if it might be taken away.

"It won't happen every morning," said Karis. "I should say that. Tomorrow you'll cite a section from memory and get its number wrong, and I shall mark it, and you'll be furious. But it's happened once, and a thing that's happened once has been shown to be possible." She brought her hands out from behind her. In one of them was a folded note, and in the other a clean sheet of the house's good paper. "Now. This."

She unfolded the note, which was in her own small hand, and short.

"That's what I wrote, at the start of the year, on the heading you found. The hearing provision. Struck in the index, never repealed. Six lines, for the counsel, in a hurry." She put it down beside the clean sheet. "I want it written properly. Where the heading stands, who struck it and how you know, the two paragraphs, every recorded use. A proper note, the kind the reading room at Greyvane would keep. And I want it under your name."

"Mine?"

"You found it. You found it by being wrong about where to look, which is how nearly everything worth having gets found. I only used it." Karis tapped the good paper. "When it's done, it goes down to the registrar, and he enters it on your file with the house's stamp: *a note upon a heading of the code, by an enrollee of the second year.* It'll sit there under your name whatever becomes of anybody else's." She said that last part quickly. "The seat has heard that provision argued twice this year. It ought to be on record somewhere who turned it up."

The girl was looking at her.

"Why would anything become of anybody else's?" she said.

It was a good question, and she had been taught to ask them, one a morning, for most of a year.

"Files change," said Karis. "It's what they're for." She took the code's second volume out of Cael's arms and set it on the corner of the table. "You'll want that. The cross-heading is in the back. Find it."

She went back across the landing to her own room. Cael followed her, and at the door he looked round. The assistant had not opened the volume yet; she was sitting with both hands still flat on the sheet that had no mark on it, reading it again.

In the reading room Karis stood for a moment with her back to him and her pale fingers pressed against the edge of the bare table.

"That's nearly everything she needs," she said. "The index, the digests, how to be wrong usefully. One more month and she won't want me for anything but arguments." She let go of the table. "I thought I'd mind that more than I do."

---

On the Fourth-day of the tenth sitting's week he fought in wing three for the last time that year.

He had known it was the last since the First-day, when the floor sheet came across the counter with a line on it that he had not seen before in two years of floor sheets. *Supervised hours under the provision, the academic year: complete upon this session.* It was in the desk clerk's hand, with the wing's stamp. The provision asked for a number of hours signed by the evaluator of record, and he had been paying them down every week since the year opened, a bout at a time. This one closed the account.

He read the rotation's line at his chalk, as he had read one before every bout since the mill town.

*Fourth-day, wing three, the Mire. Take the first late. Let him have the third. Three on boards; the fourth kept. Buys: a figure a point and a half under last week's. Spends: a knee, probably.*

He put the book away.

The Mire was a third-year, Iron Rank Five, a heavy quiet young man with a slow blink, and he stood at the north chalk with his feet planted wide. A Mire worked on the floor. He did not touch you with it; he only made it feel deeper than it was. Where he looked, the boards went soft underfoot, so that you sank to the ankle in honest pine and your next step came up slow, and while you were dragging it out he walked across and put his hand on you.

Gault turned the glass.

"Exchange," said the desk clerk.

The floor went soft under Cael's left foot at once. It was a very strange feeling, and a good piece of work: his boot told him it had gone into a hand's depth of something like wet clay.

His read told him otherwise.

Brom's gift did not care what a foot felt. It read weight and what bore it, and it came back from the boards under him as flat and dry and sound as it had been every Fifth-day for a year. There was no depth. There was a pine plank, an inch and a quarter thick, on a joist, and the clay was in his ankle and nowhere else.

He could have walked straight across it, and he knew that in the first heartbeat, as he had known what Lira's count would do before she spent it. All he had to do was believe the read and not the foot, and step as if the floor were a floor, and he would be standing at the Mire's shoulder while the Mire was still waiting for him to sink.

The rotation said *late*.

So he sank. He dragged the foot out as a man drags it out of clay, and went wide round the soft place, and was slow doing it. The Mire came across in four unhurried strides, and Cael got his forearm up in time, with the give shut, and took the palm on it and felt it go up into his shoulder like any honest Iron's.

No touch. They parted.

He spent a burst in the second, small and low, and took the touch from the side. He gave the third, as written: he let his right foot go down into a depth that was not there, and wrenched the knee a little coming out, for the look of it, and the Mire's hand was on his chest. In the fourth he found the way round, as late as he could decently leave it, and touched, and it was over.

"Two touches to one," said Gault. "Provision."

The three cards went along the trestle. The desk clerk added them, with no strike, and wrote. "Twenty-three and two-thirds."

A point and a half under last week's, and the knee ached, as he had priced it.

He stood on the pine boards in the bars of light from the high windows and waited for what he usually felt at the end of a band bout, the small sour tiredness of having done a difficult thing badly on purpose. It was there. But there was something else on top of it today, and it took him a moment to give it its name.

He was bored.

It was not quite that. He had been early; that was Lira's word for it, and she had been thinking about it for three weeks, and so had he. For the whole of four exchanges he had known what the Mire would do a half beat before the Mire did it, and had then stood and waited that half beat out, every time, so that it would look as if he had found it when a good Iron would have found it. It was a strange sort of work, for nobody had been in any danger. He had only been waiting, for a year, inside every bout.

Hiding had turned into waiting, and he had not seen it happen. There was a ruling coming, on a day he could count to, and after it either there was no more need of a band, or there was a door.

He looked toward the east end of the room.

Ephram was at the rail under the windows.

He had the three sheets out, unfolded, laid on the rail's flat top with his forearm across them against the draught, and a pencil in his hand. He was not looking at Cael; he was writing the figure in at the foot of the third sheet, in his square hand, with the date and the floor and the three cards. He set the dot beside it on the scale in the margin, and then he laid his rule across the page and drew a line under the entry, a single firm line, from edge to edge.

He had never drawn a line under one before.

He counted down the three pages with the pencil's point, not moving his lips, and looked at the red mark ruled across the top of all three, and at the two gaps, one under the other, from the weeks of the ribs.

Then he turned the third sheet over and wrote something on the back of it.

It was short. Cael could see that much from forty feet, from the way the pencil moved: four or five words, and then, after a pause in which Ephram looked out of the high window at nothing, two or three more underneath. He did not hide it; he simply wrote it facing the wall. When he had done he read it over once, and his face, which was a good plain open face that had never been much use for keeping things in, did something Cael had not seen it do before. It went still. It was the face of a man who has been adding a long column for two years and has reached the foot of it and written the sum.

He folded the sheets in four along their soft old creases and put them in the captain's file, and tied the green tape.

Cael walked the length of the room to him, since there did not seem to be anything else to do.

"That's the year," said Ephram.

"That's the year."

"Ninety-eight, and two holes." Ephram tucked the file under his arm. "And not one of them over the line. I ruled that line in the first month I knew you. I thought I'd have to move it." He looked at Cael then, level, the captain's look, the one he gave a first-year who had told him half of something. It went on a moment longer than was comfortable. "I'm not asking," he said. "I told you on your birthday I wasn't, and I'm not."

"I know."

"Good." He nodded at the pine boards, the trestle, Gault closing his case. "The Mire's a decent fighter. He'll think he gave you trouble." He started for the door, and stopped with his hand on it, and did not turn round. "He didn't, did he."

It was not a question, and he did not wait for it to be answered.

Cael stood at the rail where the sheets had lain, and the wood was still a little warm from Ephram's forearm. Whatever had been written on the back of the third page was in a green-taped file going down the wing's corridor, and he did not know what it was. He knew only that Ephram had decided something, and that it had taken him ninety-eight entries to be sure.

---

The request came to the Archmarshal's desk on a morning of sun, in the ordinary bag, and his clerk laid it in the tray on the right with the day's other paper and said nothing about it.

It was the third thing from the top, and he came to it in its turn.

A single leaf of registry stock, under the seat's seal at Ostrand, the river and the scale. He knew the form. He had answered it, in one shape or another, perhaps two hundred times in forty years, and it had never once before taken him more than the time it takes to dip a pen.

*In the matter of Caelen Hesk-ward. The evaluation seat constituted for these proceedings, having first given notice in the year's eighth week that certification would be required, now renews its request to the Archmarshal's office for certification of the standard protocol, as applied to a re-evaluation of the enrollee of record at Halcenvane, should such be ordered. The seat's closing sittings are set down for the year's last month. The office's certificate is requested before the first of them.*

Below that the form left a space, ruled with a single line, for his answer.

He read the leaf through, as he read everything, from its head to its seal.

The first notice had come in the year's eighth week and he had acknowledged it by return, in one line, as he acknowledged every notice. He remembered writing the line, and he had meant, then, to sign the certificate when it was asked for. The protocol was sound. He had helped to write two of its sections as a young man, and had defended the whole of it before three convenings, and in all his service he had never refused his name to a thing he knew to be sound.

The protocol was sound still. He could find nothing wrong with any sentence of it.

Down in the paved court the porter was feeding the pigeons from his paper bag, as he did at this hour, and the birds had come to him in their usual half-circle. The window on the Archmarshal's left stood open a hand's breadth. His left hand, which did not like mornings, lay on the blotter.

He did not open the lowest drawer. There was no finding to consult and no minute to draft, and he had known since a borrowed room in Ostrand exactly what he would write when this leaf came. He had said it aloud there, to five people, so that he would not be able to pretend afterward that he had not.

He found, now that it had come, that he did not need the saying. He would have written the same if nobody had ever heard him.

He dipped the pen.

He wrote on the ruled line, in the upright hand he kept for instruments, with no heading and no reason.

*The Archmarshal declines to certify.*

He looked at it for as long as the ink took to lose its shine. Five words. It gave no ground and cited no section and accused nobody of anything. The form had asked a question, and that was the answer to it, and there was no line on the leaf for why.

He knew what would be done with it. The code let a seat proceed upon its own authority where a certificate was withheld; he had cited that section himself, once, long ago, when a guild hall's assessor would not sign. His line would stop nothing. The sittings would sit, and the ruling would be read, and somewhere in a minute a clerk would enter that a thing usually present was not there. That was the whole of what he could do, and he had weighed it on a post road for four days and found that it was still worth doing.

He signed beneath, his name and his office, as he had signed something over nine thousand findings, and he sanded it and shook the sand back into its box.

Then he laid the leaf in the tray marked *done*.

His clerk came for the tray at the usual hour. He took up the day's finished paper against his chest, and his eyes went down the top leaf once, as a good clerk's eyes do, and stopped for the space of a breath, and went on.

"For the noon dispatch, sir?"

"With the rest," said the Archmarshal.

The clerk waited a moment, as though there might be a covering note, or a word. There was not. He went down the too-steep stair with the tray, and the Archmarshal heard him go, every step, to the bottom.

He drew the next thing toward him from the tray on the right. It was a guild hall's query about a frame. He read it through from its head to its seal, and dipped his pen, and answered it.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-06-the-compacts-hand/manuscript/chapter-47.md

# Chapter 47 — The Last Month

The Log for those weeks was short, because the weeks were all the same.

He wrote it at the sill, most nights, by the lamp, with Hesk's clock losing its minutes on the shelf behind him and the court dark below. He wrote less than he had ever written in a term, for there was a great deal he could not put in a book, and what was left over was so much alike from one Fifth-day to the next that he caught himself, more than once, turning back a leaf to make sure he had not already entered it.

*Fifth-day. Ninth sitting. Forty minutes by the exchange's clock. The house's counsel on the remedy: that no seat has ever before ordered a practitioner into a frame over a provision that stands. Jent: that the code allows it, and the petition asks for nothing the code doesn't give. Both true. The hood wrote for some time and said nothing. We came home.*

*Seventh-day. The lodge, with Seln. The road column: none, none, the post, a carter with slates for the stable roof. Four coats. He says the bundle under his table is as thick now as his hand is wide, and that he'll write another on the next Seventh-day.*

*First-day. Rooke calls the foot after she's in the air. Lira says it's like being asked your name while you're falling downstairs. She gets it right nine in ten. She's stopped asking it for leave. She told me so, and then showed me, and then told me again.*
